UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
EASTERN DISTRICT OF VIRGINIA

BOBBY LEE RAMDASS,

RONALD J. ANGELONE, Director of the

MAGISTRATE JUDGE’S REPORT 
AND RECOMMENDATION 


This matter was initiated by petition for writ of 
habeas corpus under 28 U.S.C. § 2254. The matter was 
referred to the undersigned United States Magistrate 
Judge pursuant to the provisions of 28 U.S.C. 
§ 636(b)(1)(B) and (C) and Rule 28 of the Rules of the 
United States District Court for the Eastern District of 


Virginia. 


I. STATEMENT OF THE CASE 
A. Background 


Petitioner is before the Court seeking a writ of habeas 
corpus as a result of his convictions in the Circuit Court 
of Fairfax County, Virginia, of capital murder, robbery, 
and use of a firearm in the commission of a felony, all of 
which occurred in the course of an armed robbery on 
September 2, 1992, during which Mohammad Z. Kayani, 
a convenience store clerk, was slain. Petitioner was tried 


192 


in January, 1993, pled guilty to the robbery and was 
found guilty of the murder and use of a firearm charges 
following a jury trial. Petitioner was sentenced to death 
for the murder and to terms of imprisonment for the 
remaining offenses. All sentences imposed by the jury 
were ordered by the Court on April 2, 1993. 


B. Facts 


The Court's decision is driven by the facts set forth in 
the record. A significant portion of the factual summary 
which follows has been taken from the opinion of Vir- 
ginia Supreme Court Justice Whiting denying petitioner's 
claims on direct appeal. Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 437 
S.E.2d 566, 568 (Va. 1993). Additional matters relevant to 
the issues before the Court have been added as appropri- 
ate, both here and in the body of the opinion, to complete 
the context in which the petition has been considered. 


At the outset, it is important to note that the trier of 
fact may not impose the death penalty in the absence of a 
finding beyond a reasonable doubt that (1) “there is a 
probability based upon evidence of the prior history of 
the defendant or of the circumstances surrounding the 
commission of the offense of which he is accused that he 
would commit criminal acts of violence that would con- 
stitute a continuing serious threat to society,” or (2) “that 
his conduct in committing the offense was outrageously 
or wantonly vile, horrible or inhuman, in that it involved 
torture, depravity of mind or aggravated battery to the 
victim.” Va. Code § 19.2-264.4(C). 


193 


Following petitioner’s conviction, but prior to the 
penalty phase, the prosection [sic] conceded that it was 
not entitled to proceed under the second part of the 
statute (vileness) but only to “proceed[ ] under the alter- 
native theory of future dangerousness. . . . ” (1/29/93 Tr. 
at 8.) 


Between August 25, 1992, and September 2, 1992, 
petitioner and several associates, two of whom testified at 
his trial, committed four armed robberies. During one of 
the robberies, petitioner pistol-whipped the night clerk at 
the hotel being robbed; the following night, he shot a cab 
driver in the head, leaving him to die. The cab driver 
lived to testify at trial. Two days later, the predicate 
offenses occurred. 


During the night of September 1, 1992, and early 
morning of September 2, 1992, petitioner and Darrell 
Wilson, both armed with pistols, were returning home in 
a car with three other men, Shane Singh, Edward O’Con- 
nor, and Candelerio Ramirez, after abandoning a plan to 
rob persons at a Roy Rogers restaurant in Fairfax County. 
The restaurant was closed. Instead, the men, including 
petitioner, decided that they would rob persons at a 
7-Eleven store on Buelah Street in Fairfax County. 


Near one o'clock on the morning of September 2, 
1992, the five men entered the 7-Eleven store. Petitioner 
entered first and “drew” his pistol on Kayani, a clerk who 
was behind the cash register. Wilson, who also displayed 
his pistol, ordered all the customers to lie on the floor and 
not look at him. The other three men, who were unarmed, 


194 


took the customers’ wallets, money from the cash regis- 
ter, cigarettes and lottery tickets from the store’s stock, 
and Kool-Aid. 


After petitioner ordered Kayani to open the safe, 
Kayani knelt down next to the safe and unsuccessfully 
tried to open it. Petitioner squatted next to Kayani and 
yelled at him to open the safe “or I'll blow your f - - head 
off.1” Ramdass, 437 S.E.2d at 568. At approximately the 
same time, Wilson fired his pistol at one of the customers 
lying on the floor. (1/27/93 Tr. at 206-07.) Immediately 
thereafter, Singh, standing behind petitioner, saw peti- 
tioner shoot Kayani in the head on his second attempt to 
get the weapon to fire. (1/2. /93 Tr. at 211-12.) Testifying 
about the precise moment of the shooting, Singh stated 
that petitioner “[c]licked it once and it didn’t go off, 
clicked it again and it went off.” (Id.) 


Just after petitioner shot Kayani, Ramirez returned 
from a back room in the store. Ramirez saw petitioner 
laughing as he stood over Kayani’s body. (1/27/93 Tr. at 
145.) Later, Ramirez heard petitioner say that he shot 
Kayani because he “took too long.” (1/27/93 Tr. at 147.) 
Shortly thereafter, Ramirez opened the front door and 
Wilson, Singh, and O’Connor ran out. As Ramirez held 
the door open, he urged petitioner to “[clome on.” (Id. at 
146.) However, petitioner was “clicking the gun at the 
people on the floor” and told Ramirez to “[s}hut up or I'll 
put one in you.” (Id.) One of the customers also heard the 


1 Another employee of the 7-Eleven store testified that a 
clerk could not open the part of the safe containing most of the 
money because the store manager had the keys. 


195 


clicking of the gun as petitioner left. (1/27/93 Tr. at 125, 
129-30.) 


When they ge’ in the car, Ramirez heard petitioner 
ask Wilson, “[wJhy didn’t you get rid of the people on the 
floor?” (Id. at 146.) After the men divided the robbery 
proceeds at Singh’s home, petitioner told Ramirez, 
“[djon’t tell anybody about this [o}r I'll kill you and I'll 
kill your whole family.” (1/27/93 Tr. at 146-47.) 


Singh, a co-owner of the gun with petitioner, testified 
that the gun would not fire unless held at a certain angle 
because the bullets in the cylinder were not the right size 
for the gun. Singh was corroborated by Julian Jay Mason, 
Jr., a forensic scientist specializing in firearms identifica- 
tion, who later examined and test-fired the gun. (Id. at 
247.) Mason testified that the 9 millimeter cartridges peti- 
tioner used in the gun were smaller than the .38 caliber 
cartridges designed for the gun. Therefore, when the 
gun’s muzzle was pointed down, the 9 millimeter car- 
tridges slid too far forward in the cylinder to be struck by 
the firing pin. (Id. at 250-51.) Mason further testified that 
when the muzzle was pointed up, cartridges slid back 
closer to the firing pin, and the gun could be fired. (Id. at 
251.) 


The Commonwealth, in seeking the death penalty, 
only relied upon the “continuing serious threat to soci- 
ety” condition, also known as “future dangerousness.” In 
support of its position, the Commonwealth introduced 
evidence of petitioner’s use of a firearm and participation 
in the robbery of a Pizza Hut on August 25, 1992; robbery 
on August 29, 1992, of a hotel night clerk, who was 
physically beaten by petitioner and threatened with a gun 


196 


during the robbery; use of a firearm and robbery of a 
Domino’s Pizza restaurant on August 30, 1992; and a 
second robbery and attempted murder, on August 39, 
1992, of a taxi driver, who was shot in the head by 
petitioner. At the time of trial, petitioner had been con- 
victed of the Pizza Hut crimes and the Domino’s Pizza 
crimes and formally sentenced for the Pizza Hut robbery. 
At the time judgment was imposed by the court for the 
7-Eleven crimes, petitioner had additionally been con- 
victed and sentenced for the hotel robbery and formally 
sentenced for the Domino’s robbery. 


While all of the above described offenses occurred 
within a week of the murder of Kayani, the jury was also 
made aware of petitioner’s troubled history. 


The record reveals that the jury was informed of 
petitioner’s extensive juvenile record, beginning in 1985, 
with a purse snatching, for which he received probation, 
(1/29/93 Tr. at 198-99); theft of a moped and a burglary, 
for which he was sentenced to a juvenile facility, 
(1/29/93 Tr. at 200); being a juvenile escapee, (1/29/93 
Tr. at 201); and robbery as a juvenile, for which he was 
sentenced to Southampton prison as an adult, (1/29/93 
Tr. at 17-18, 202). The jury also learned that petitioner 
acquired his first gun at age thirteen or fourteen, while he 
was selling drugs, (1/29/93 Tr. at 149-51); that he was in 
possession of a loaded gun when he was arrested, as a 
juvenile, and charged with robbery, (1/29/93 Tr. at 24); 
and that he violated his parole, which began on May 21, 
1992, by acquiring a gun on July 4, 1992, less than sixty 
days later, (1/29/93 Tr. at 85-86, 202). 


197 


To offset the elaborate prosecution effort to establish 
petitioner’s future danger to society, the defense pre- 
sented evidence of a bleak, abusive, unstructured child- 
hood, during which petitioner was physically and 
emotionally abused, raised in conditions of severe depri- 
vation and in the complete absence of any positive guid- 
ance. 


Petitioner’s brother, Mark Ramdass, was called on 
petitioner’s behalf. After admitting that he would not 
have appeared without being subpoenaed, Ramdass 
described his brother’s efforts to protect him and try to 
keep him out of trouble. (1/29/93 Tr. at 103-05.) He also 
acknowledged his brother’s numerous encounters with 
the law and incarceration, and admitted his own criminal 
activities, resulting in two felony convictions. (Id. at 112.) 
At one time, petitioner and the witness were both at 
Southampton Correctional facility. (Id. at 113.) 


Jennifer Joffe, a Virginia probation/parole officer, 
was called by the defense. She prepared the probation 
report, following petitioner’s 1988 conviction for robbery. 
(Id. at 115.) Joffe presented evidence of petitioner’s home 
environment and difficult upbringing. On cross-examina- 
tion, she acknowledged that none of the counseling 
reports prepared on petitioner revealed the presence of 
psychoses or serious mental problems. (Id. at 126.) She 
also concurred in the assessment of petitioner as imma- 
ture, impulsive, unremorseful, and possessed of a great 
potential for violence. (Id. at 127.) In response to the 
question “[y]ou felt he was a danger on the street,” she 
responded, “[y]es.” (Id. at 128.) 


198 


Petitioner’s mother testified that she was one of 
twenty-one children and that she was sexually abused 
and ran away from home at an early age. (Id. at 217.) She 
was working as a topless dancer when petitioner was 
born. (Id. at 219.) She acknowledged being convicted of 
writing a bad check and two assault and battery charges, 
and confirmed that she twice lost custody of her children 
for neglect. (Id. at 227-28.) She described how petitioner 
was physically abused by a boyfriend and how she 
taught petitioner to defend himself. She admitted con- 
tracting venereal disease from another live-in boyfriend 
and admitted assaulting a former boyfriend and a neigh- 
bor. (Id. at 232, 228-30.) 


Finally, the following exchange took place between 
defense counsel and petitioner’s mother: 


Q. Do you realize that Bobby could be exe- 
cuted? 

A. Yes. 

Q. How do you feel about that? 

A. I don’t know. 

Q. He’s your son. How do you feel about the 
fact that he could die? 

A. I just don’t know. I just - I’m not feeling 
anything right now. 

Q. Did you ever teach Bobby not to cry? 

A. Yes. 


Q. Did you teach him not to show any emo- 
tion? 


199 


A. Yes, I taught him that, too. 
Q. Did you show any emotion? 
A. Not lately, no, not for a long time. 


(4/2/93 Tr. at 10-11). 


The factual issues addressing petitioner’s future 
threat to society and the mitigating factors offered to 
explain, in part, petitioner’s conduct were thoroughly 
explored. 


C. Procedural History 


On September 2, 1992, petitioner murdered Muham- 


mad Kayani, a clerk employed at a 7-Eleven convenience 
store on Buelah Street in Fairfax County, Virginia. He was 
arrested on September 11, 1992, for an earlier robbery, 
together with other defendants, and ultimately connected 
to the 7-Eleven robbery and murder. On September 21, 
1992, petitioner was indicted by a Fairfax County grand 
jury on capital murder, armed robbery, and use of a 
firearm in the commission of a robbery. 


Counsel was appointed in timely fashion, pretrial 
discovery was promptly pursued, and the matter came on 
for trial on January 26, 1993. At trial, petitioner, by coun- 
sel, entered a plea of guilty to the robbery charge which 
was accepted by the court following the normal colloquy. 
However, at the request of the Commonwealth Attorney, 
and over the objection of defense counsel, a finding with 
regard to Count II of the indictments, robbery, was with- 
held pending the receipt of evidence of the other 
offenses. ; 


200 


At the conclusion of the evidential portion of the 
trial, the jury returned a verdict of guilty of capital mur- 
der and guilty of use of a firearm in the commission of a 
felony, for which petitioner was sentenced to four years 
in the penitentiary. Following the jury verdict, the court 
found petitioner guilty with respect to Count II, robbery, 
finding on the record that petitioner’s plea of guilty was 
freely and voluntarily given. The defense waived prepa- 
ration of a presentence report, but the court, sua sponte, 
ordered the preparation of a presentence report on the 
offense of robbery. Thereafter, the jury heard evidence 
with regard to punishment on the capital murder charge, 
and following argument and instruction by the court, 
returned a verdict of death. 


In anticipation of preparation of the presentence 
report, the matter was set over for further proceedings on 
March 3, 1993. However, sentencing was continued from 
that date until April 2, 1993, at which time the court 
imposed judgment upon petitioner, sentencing him to 
death in conformity with the jury verdict for the offense 
of capital murder; four years in the penitentiary, in con- 
formity with the jury verdict, for use of a firearm in the 
commission of a felony; and life for the robbery, incident 
to petitioner’s plea of guilty. 


While the jury was deliberating on punishment dur- 
ing the penalty phase of the capital murder proceeding, 
they submitted a question to the court stated as follows: 
“{I)}f the defendant is given life, is there a possibility of 
parole at some time before his natural death,” (1/29/93 
Tr. at 278), to which the court responded: “[I]f you find 
the Defendant guilty, which you have in this case, you 
should impose such punishment as you feel is just under 


201 


the evidence and within the instructions of the Court. 


You are not to concern yourselves with what may happen 
afterwards.” (1/29/93 Tr. at 281.) 


Trial counsel pursued a direct appeal on behalf of 
petitioner, assigning the following errors: 


1. The Trial Court erred in denying the Appel- 
lant’s Motion For A Bill of Particulars, thereby 
denying Appellant his rights protected by the 
Sixth and Fourteenth Amendments of The 
United States Constitution and the Constitution 
of Virginia. 


2. The Trial Court erred, and denied the Appel- 
lant his Fifth, Sixth and Fourteenth Amendment 
rights, and his Rights protected by The Virginia 
Constitution through the denial of his motion 
for discovery of co-defendant polygraph ques- 
tions and answers for impeachment purposes. 


3. The Trial Court erred in granting an 
extended reasonable doubt instruction. 


4. The Trial Court erred in denying the jury 
instruction utilized in Commonwealth v. Wright, 
tried in the same circuit and approved by the 
Virginia Supreme Court. 


5. The Trial Court erred in denying the defen- 
dant’s proffered jury instruction regarding the 
inconsistent testimony of Shane Singh. 


6. The Trial Court erred in admitting evidence 
of the Defendant's unadjudicated misconduct in 
violation of the United States and Virginia Con- 
stitutions. 


7. The Trial Court erred in denying the Defen- 
dant’s proffered sentencing jury instruction 
“B-S” which instructed the jury that they must 


202 


all agree that the Defendant would be a future 
danger. 


8. The Trial Court erred, and denied Defendant 
his rights protected by the Virginia and United 
States Constitutions 5th and 14th Amendment 
Rights, by denying Defendant's proffered sen- 
tencing jury instruction “E-S” which instructed 
they [sic] jury as to the standard of proof 
required to be convinced of the unadjudicated 
misconduct of the Defendant. 


9. The Trial Court erred in refusing to respond 
to a jury question, during the sentencing stage 
of the trial, that Defendant would not be eligible 
for parole upon his conviction of capital murder 
in the instant matter in violation §19.2-264.4 of 
the Virginia Code ard the Eighth and Four- 
teenth Amendments to the United States Consti- 
tution. 


10. The Trial Court erred in denying Defendant 
the right to raise his inability to make parole 
upon conviction of the capital murder in the 
instant case. 


11. The imposition of the death penalty by 
electrocution is cruel and unusual punishment, 
in violation cf the Virginia Constitution and the 
Eighth Amendment of the United States Consti- 
tution and the Trial Court erred in finding to the 
contrary. 


12. The Trial Court erred in withholding a 
finding of guilt on the underlying robbery 
charge, and further erred, and denied the Defen- 
dant’s 5th and 14th Amendment rights by refus- 
ing to sentence the Defendant without a 
presentencing report despite its waiver by 
Defendant and his counsel and the fact that the 


203 


Defendant had been incarcerated the entire 
period of time since the preparation of the prior 
presentencing report. 


13. The Trial Court erred in denying the defen- 
dant funds for expert witnesses, medical doc- 
tors, private investigators. 


14. The Trial Court erred in denying the Defen- 
dant a medical expert to perform a competent 
physical examination to ascertain his physical 
disabilities. 


15. The Court erred in denying the Defendant 
discovery regarding future dangerousness prior 
to trial. 


16. The Trial Court erred in denying Defen- 
dant’s Motion to Dismiss on the basis that the 
Virginia Death Penalty Statute is unconstitu- 
tional. 


17. The Trial Court erred in denying defen- 
dant’s motion to dismiss on the basis of the 
denial of due process under the United States 
and Virginia Constitutions. 


18. The Trial Court erred in refusing to dismiss 
a juror who was personally acquainted with one 
of the Commonwealth (police) witnesses, and an 
additional juror who had preconceived notions 
regarding the brutality of the crime. 


19. The Trial Court erred and violated his 
Fifth, Eighth and Fourteenth Amendment Rights 
in refusing to permit inquiry into whether the 
jurors held the belief that the Defendant would 
be executed if the jury were to impose the death 
sentence. 


20. The Trial Court erred in denying Defendant 
the opportunity to voir dire the jury regarding 


204 


specific mitigation circumstances about which 
they would be generally instructed, and further 
erred in denying Defendant’s motion to exclude 
those jurors that were unable to consider speci- 
fic mitigating circumstances in sentencing. 


21. The Trial Court erred in denying the Defen- 
dant’s motion for in-camera requests for expert 
witnesses in violation of his Fourth, Fifth, Sixth 
and Fourteenth Amendment rights of the United 
States Constitution and rights protected by the 
Virginia Constitution. 


22. The Trial Court erred in denying Defen- 
dant’s motion for a private investigator to aid 
the defense in the preparation of trial in viola- 
tion of his Fifth and Fourteenth Amendment 
Rights and his rights protected by the Virginia 
Constitution. 


23. The Trial Court erred in denying the Defen- 
dant’s motion that it review evidence to deter- 
mine whether evidence was or was not 
exculpatory rather than leaving such determina- 
tion to the Commonwealth Attorney in violation 
of his Sixth and Fourteenth Amendment Rights 
and rights protected by the Virginia Constitu- 
tion and in violation of the discovery rules of 
this court. 


24. The Trial Court erred in permitting hearsay 
testimony, regarding personal information about 
the victim, via the first page of the medical 
examiners reports without redacting hearsay 
portions. 


25. The Trial Court erred in sustaining objec- 
tions regarding the testimony about the lack of 
fingerprints left by Mr. Ramirez in the 7-11; as to 
the specific location of Mr. Ramirez on dates 


205 


preceding this incident; in denying the Defen- 
dant’s probing into the background of Common- 
wealth witness Mr. Ramirez, and in denying the 
defendant the opportunity to examine Mr. Ram- 
irez and Mr. Singh regarding other offenses in 
which the witnesses were involved thereby 
denying him right of confrontation in violation 
of the Sixth Amendment of the Constitution and 
rights protected by the Virginia Constitution. 


On November 5, 1993, the conviction was affirmed. 
Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 437 S.E.2d 566 (Va. 1993). The 
court noted that Assignments 7, 17, 18, 19, 20, and 24 
were not briefed and concluded that those issues had 
been waived. With regard to the remaining issues, the 
court addressed and ruled on the issues on the merits. 


Following denial of the appeal, a petition for cer- 
tiorari was filed in the United States Supreme Court. 
While the petition addressed multiple issues, including 
discovery, unadjudicated misconduct, constitutionality of 
the death sentence, expert assistance, and potentially 
exculpatory evidence, only one assignment was signifi- 
cant: “Whether the Court’s refusal to respond to [the] 
jury inquiry as to Defendant’s parole status violates the 
Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth Amendments of the U.S. 
Constitution.” 


There was no action for several months, but on June 
17, 1994, the United States Supreme Court decided the 
case of Simmons v. South Carolina, 512 U.S. 154 (1994), and 
ruled that where a defendant's future dangerousness is at 
issue and state law prohibits a defendant's release on 
parole, due process requires the sentencing jury to be 
informed that the defendant is parole ineligible. 


206 


Three days later, on June 20, 1994, certiorari in peti- 
tioner’s case was granted and the case was remanded to 
the Virginia Supreme Court for further consideration 
based upon the Supreme Court’s opinion in Simmons. 
Ramdass v. Virginia, 512 U.S. 1217 (1994). 


As presented by petitioner to the Supreme Court of 
Virginia on remand, the sole issue in Simmons was 
“[w]hether the due process clause of the Fourteenth 
Amendment was violated by the refusal of the trial judge 
to instruct the jury that the defendant was ineligible for 
parole when the prosecution relied on future dangerous- 
ness to justify imposition of the death penalty.” Respon- 
dent argued that Simmons was not raised in the trial 
court; that petitioner was ineligible for parole and Sim- 
mons, therefore, did not apply; and that Simmons was 
irrelevant because the sentencer can always consider 
parole ineligibility. 


On November 4, 1994, the Supreme Court rejected 
petitioner’s claim, found that petitioner was ineligible for 
parole, and concluded that Simmons did not apply. Ram- 
dass v. Commonwealth, 450 S.E.2d 360 (Va. 1994). 


Once again a petition for writ of certiorari was filed 
in the Supreme Court of the United States, raising many 
of the same issues originally presented, but the petition 
was rejected on April 24, 1995. Ramdass v. Virginia, 514 
U.S. 1085 (1995). 


On May 11, 1995, counsel was appointed to pursue a 
petition for writ of habeas corpus in the courts of the 
Commonwealth. 


207 


On August 30, 1995, petitioner instituted a petition 
for writ of habeas corpus in the Virginia Supreme Court 
alleging the following broad categories as grounds for 
relief: 


1. Denial of effective assistance of counsel for 
reasons addressing trial strategy (or lack 
thereof), improper voir dire, venue, jury 
sequestration, handling of defense experts, 
handling of evidence, improper preparation 
for examination of prosecution experts, 
inadequate pretrial investigation, improper 
handling of a co-defendant’s testimony, 
objections addressing the murder weapon 
and the forensics expert, and improper han- 
dling of the sentencing phase. 


2. Prosecutorial misconduct, consisting of the 
withholding of exculpatory evidence; use of 
highly prejudicial evidence (in the form of 
photos, the autopsy report, unadjudicated 
misconduct, and a co-defendant’s sentence); 
and improper evidence regarding parole. 


3. Trial court error in the form of a delayed 
finding of guilt on the robbery charge; fail- 
ure to strike members of the jury panel chal- 
lenged for cause; failure to require the 
prosecution to produce certain purportedly 
exculpatory evidence; admission of evidence 
that was either highly prejudicial or not pre- 
ceded by a proper foundation; instructional 
errors, a Simmons error; and the denial of an 
investigator. 


4. The Virginia Supreme Court proportionality 
review was inadequate. 


208 


An ex parte motion for expert assistance and an inves- 
tigator was denied on January 19, 1996, and the habeas 
petition was denied on March 18, 1996, without an evi- 
dentiary hearing. 


On April 19, 1996, counsel filed a petition for rehear- 
ing in the state habeas proceeding, alleging as grounds 
for the rehearing that petitioner was denied the effective 
assistance of counsel (for a variety of reasons previously 
presented), that the prosecution engaged in misconduct 
prejudicial to petitioner, and that the trial court denied 
petitioner’s constitutional rights in numerous respects. 
Some issues were raised for the first time, and the Sim- 
mons claim is well articulated. The petition for rehearing 
was denied June 7, 1996. 


Following denial of the petition for rehearing, the 
Circuit Court of Fairfax County set petitioner’s execution 
for August 28, 1996. 


On June 25, 1996, a motion by petitioner to stay 
execution was filed, but the motion was denied by the 
Virginia Supreme Court on July 17, 1996. 


On August 14, 1996, incident to plans to present a 
federal habeas petition, petitioner moved for a stay of 
execution and for leave to proceed in forma pauperis, and 
for appointment of counsel in federal court. 


On August 15, 1996, the execution was stayed. There- 
after, on September 11, 1996, counsel was appointed for 
the purpose of filing the current action. 


On February 14, 1997, the present petition was filed. 
On March 11, 1997, an amended petition was filed, and 
that is the pleading upon which all further actions have 


209 


been based. The amended petition presents, in the broad- 
est possible terms, the Simmons issue; claims of denial of 
effective assistance of counsel; claims based on the 
alleged denial of a mental health expert and other 
experts; the denial of exculpatory material sought from 
the prosecution; the absence of due process because the 
Virginia sentencing statute is unconstitutional on its face; 
that the death penalty statute is unconstitutional on its 
face; and that prejudicial errors of a constitutional dimen- 
sion were committed during the penalty phase. 


A response to the petition and a motion to dismiss 
were filed on behalf of the respondent on April 28, 1997. 


On June 3, 1997, petitioner’s response to the motion 
to dismiss was filed. 


On June 26, 1997, the respondent’s reply brief was 
filed. 


The issue are [sic] now ripe for adjudication.

request for relief under 28 U.S.C. § 2254. For ease of

next to the Court’s numbering. Subparts of major topics

claim, e.g. A.2.a. (the Virginia Supreme Court rejection of 


the Simmons claim on remand was ultra vires) or consti- 
tutes a separate and independent claim, e.g. C.5.a. (fail-

ure of trial counsel to conduct adequate voir dire).

Il. FINDINGS OF FACT AND CONCLUSIONS OF 


A. Motion to Dismiss 


In ruling on a motion to dismiss for failure to state a 
claim upon which relief can be granted, the complaint is 
construed in the light most favorable to the plaintiffs and 
their allegations are taken as true. Scheuer v. Rhodes, 416 
U.S. 232, 236 (1974); Jenkins v. McKeithen, 395 U.S. 411, 421 
(1969). The complaint should not be dismissed unless it 
appears to a certainty that the plaintiff can prove no facts 
in support of his claim which would entitle him to relief. 
Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S. 41, 45-46 (1957); Martin Marietta 
Corp. v. International Telecommunications Satellite Org., 991 
F.2d 94, 97 (4th Cir. 1993); Bruce v. Riddle, 631 F.2d 272, 
273-74 (4th Cir. 1980). The courts must construe the com- 
plaint liberally in favor of the plaintiffs, even if recovery 
appears remote and unlikely. See Jenkins, 395 U.S. at 421. 
In ruling on a 12(b)(6) motion, the court primarily con- 
siders the allegations in the complaint but may consider 
attached exhibits and documents incorporated by refer- 
ence. See Simons v. Montgomery County Police Officers, 762 
F.2d 30, 31 (4th Cir. 1985), cert. denied, 474 U.S. 1054 
(1986); Wolford v. Budd Co., 149 F.R.D. 127, 129-32 (W.D. 
Va. 1993). 


B. Standard of Review in Habeas Corpus Proceed- 
ings. 

In 1996, Congress enacted, and the President signed 

into law, the Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty 

Act of 1996, Pub.L. 104-132, 110 Stat, 1214 (April 24, 1996) 


215 


(hereinafter “AEDPA”). The AEDPA provided habeas 
reform in both capital and noncapital cases. Section 107 
of the AEDPA added a new chapter to Title 28 establish- 
ing special procedures in capital cases. 28 U.S.C. 
§§ 2261-2265. Among them was the establishment of a 
six-month statute of limitation for the filing of a petition 
and a further limitation within which to act on any such 
application. 


This Court has previously determined that section 
107 of the AEDPA does not apply in this case because “on 
the date Ramdass’s state habeas counsel was appointed, 
Virginia was not in compliance with the requirements of 
the AEDPA because it did not have a mandatory appoint- 
ment mechanism,” nor did it meet the federal statutory 
requirements for compensation and payment of reason- 
able litigation expenses for counsel appointed to indigent 
death row inmates and only arguably met the compe- 
tency standards. Ramdass v. Netherland, 2:96CV831, Order 
of December 6, 1996. Similar rulings have been arrived at 
in Wright v. Angelone, 944 F. Supp. 460 (E.D. Va. 1996); See 
also Satcher v. Netherland, 944 F. Supp. 1222, 1238 (E.D. Va. 
1996); Breard v. Netherland, 949 F. Supp. 1255, 1261-62 
(E.D. Va. 1966). 


However, section 104 of the AEDPA, which amends 
28 U.S.C. § 2254, governing noncapital habeas claims, 
does apply. As the Supreme Court noted in Lindh v. 
Murphy, 117 S.Ct. 2059 (1997), “[t]he statute reveals Con- 
gress’s intent to apply the amendments to chapter 153 [28 
U.S.C. § 2241 et seq.] only to such cases as were filed after 
the statute’s enactment. .. . ” Id. at 2063. Justice Rehn- 
quist goes on to say “[w]e read . . . section 107(c) . . . as 
indicating implicitly that the amendments to chapter 153 


216 


were assumed and meant to apply to the general run of 


habeas cases only when those cases had been filed after 
the date of the Act.” Id. 


In Mu’Min v. Pruett, 125 F.3d 192 (4th Cir. 1997) and 
O'dell v. Netherland, 95 F.3d 1214 (4th Cir. 1996) the Fourth 
Circuit declined to apply the provisions of the AEDPA 
because each habeas petition was filed prior to the effec- 
tive date of the statute. Thus, litigants in those cases were 
entitled to the benefit of the pre-AEDPA standards. 


Petitioner, however, is governed by “the considerably 


more demanding standards . . . imposed on habeas peti- 
tioners” by the AEDPA. O’dell, 95 F.3d at 1255 n.36. 


As applied to petitioner, 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d) reads 


An application for a writ of habeas us on 
behalf of a person in custody sunnmen te the 
judgment of a State court shall not be granted 
with respect to any claim that was adjudicated 
on the merits in State court proceedings unless 
the adjudication of the claim - (1) resulted in a 
decision that was contrary to, or involved an 
unreasonable application of, clearly established 
Federal law, as determined by the Supreme 
Court of the United States; or (2) resulted in a 
decision that was based on an unreasonable 
determination of the facts in light of the evi- 
dence presented in the State court proceeding. 


28 U.S.C. § 2254(d). 


The language of the statute is important; the legisla- 
tive “and’s” and “or’s” must be clearly understood 
because for petitioner to prevail, he must establish that 
his claim was adjudicated on the merits in state court 


217 


proceedings and resulted in a decision and that the deci- 
sion was contrary to or involved an unreasonable appli- 
cation of clearly established federal law; or that his claim 
was adjudicated on the merits in a state court proceeding 
and resulted in a decision, and the decision was based on 
an unreasonable determination of the facts in light of the 
evidence presented in the state court proceeding. 


Because many legal conclusions arise out of a specific 
factual context, 28 U.S.C. § 2254(e)(1) is particularly 
important: 


In a proceeding instituted by an application for 
a writ of habeas corpus by a person in custody 
pursuant to the judgment of a State court, a 
determination of a factual issue made by a State 
court shall be presumed to be correct. The appli- 
cant shall have the burden of rebutting the presump- 
tion of correctness by clear and convincing evidence. 


28 U.S.C. § 2254(e)(1) (emphasis added). 


To underscore the deference intended to be accorded 
the factual findings of a state court, the statute goes on to 
provide that when an applicant has failed to develop the 
factual basis of a claim in state court proceedings, an 
evidential hearing “shall not” be conducted unless a peti- 
tioner establishes that the claim relies on a new, retroac- 
tively-applied constitutional rule, that the fact could not 
be previously discovered with due diligence or that “the 
facts underlying the claim would be sufficient to establish 
by clear and convincing evidence that but for constitu- 
tional error, no reasonable factfinder would have found 
the applicant guilty of the underlying offense.” 28 U.S.C. 


§ 2254(e)(2)(B). 


218 


C. Petitioner’s Claims are Exhausted and are Sub- 
ject to Federal Review. 


An application for a writ of habeas corpus on 
behalf of a person in custody pursuant to the 
judgment of a State court shall not be granted 
unless it appears that - (A) the applicant has 
exhausted the remedies available in the courts 
of the State; or (B)(I) there is an absence of 
available State corrective process; or (ii) circum- 
stances exist that render such process ineffective 
to protect the rights of the applicant. 


28 U.S.C. § 2254(b)(1). 


The exhaustion requirement dictates that a petitioner 
must first present his claims for relief to state courts 
before a petition for habeas corpus may be granted by the 
federal courts. A claim raised in a federal petition for writ 
of habeas corpus must be essentially the same claim as 
that presented in state proceedings. Pitchess v. Davis, 421 
U.S. 482 (1975); Picard v. Conner, 404 U.S. 270, 275-76 
(1971). See also Anderson v. Harless, 459 U.S. 4, 6 (1982); 
Satcher v. Pruett, 126 F.3d 561, 573 (4th Cir. 1997); Mu’Min 
v. Pruett, 125 F.3d 192, 197-98 (4th Cir. 1997); Duncan v. 
Henry, 115 S.Ct. 887, 888 (1995). 


Not all of petitioner’s claims were raised on direct 
appeal or in his state habeas action. However, for pur- 
poses of the exhaustion requirement, all of petitioner’s 
claims are deemed to be exhausted because Virginia stat- 
utes, rules, and case law preclude raising issues for the 
first time, with certain notable exceptions (i.e. ineffective 
assistance of trial counsel) in a habeas proceeding. 


219 


Because any claims presented by petitioner which 
were not raised on direct appeal are barred by the con- 
temporaneous objection requirement of Rule 5:25, Rules 
of the Supreme Court, and applied through Slayton v. 
Parrigan, 205 S.E.2d 680 (Va. 1974), and because any 
issues not raised in petitioner’s original state habeas peti- 
tion may not be considered in any subsequent petition, 
the claims are considered both exhausted and pro- 
cedurally barred. Va. Code Ann. § 8.01-654(B)(2); Gray v. 
Netherland, 116 S.Ct. 2074, 2080 (1996); Teague v. Lane, 489 
U.S. 288, 297-98 (1989); Castille v. Peoples, 489 U.S. 346, 351 
(1989); Bassette v. Thompson, 915 F.2d 932, 937 (4th Cir. 
1990); Ferguson v. Cox, 464 F.2d 461, 463 (4th Cir. 1972). 


Respondent concedes that “[ajll . . . claims are 
exhausted within the meaning of 28 U.S.C. § 2254(b) 
.,” and the Court agrees. 


D. Some of Petitioner’s Claims are Procedurally 
Defaulted 


Under Virginia law, a claim included in a petition for 
* habeas corpus will be barred if an objection was not 
raised at trial and the objection presented on direct 
appeal. In Coppola v. Warden of Virginia State Penitentiary, 
282 S.E.2d 10 (Va. 1981), the Supreme Court of Virginia 
held that to preserve an issue for appeal, and for a habeas 
corpus proceeding, the issue must be timely objected to at 
trial. Rule 5:25 of the Supreme Court of Virginia states the 
contemporaneous objection rule: “Error will not be sus- 
tained to any ruling of the trial court or the commission 


220 


before which the case was initially tried unless the objec- 
tion was stated with reasonable certainty at the time of 
the ruling... .” Va. S.Ct. R. 5:25. 


The Supreme Court has stated that: “Under Virginia 
law, failure to raise a claim on direct appeal from a 
criminal conviction ordinarily bars consideration of that 
claim in any subsequent state proceeding.” Smith v. Mur- 
ray, 477 U.S. 527, 533 (1986). The Fourth Circuit has held 
that if a claim is procedurally barred in Virginia courts 
because it was not brought on direct appeal, it will also 
be barred in the federal system. Bassette v. Thompson, 915 
F.2d 932 (4th Cir. 1990).2 In Whitley v. Bair, 802 F.2d 1487 
(4th Cir. 1986), the court held that the failure of an inmate 


to directly appeal his conviction to the Supreme Court 
deprives 


the Virginia Supreme Court of the opportunity 
to rule on the merits of his claims. We consider 
such failure to constitute a violation of the 
requirements of Rule 5:21, which applies to 
appeals of all Virginia cases, civil or criminal, 
and conclude that such violation constitutes a 
procedural default sufficient to preclude federal 
court review of the merits... . 


Id. at 1502 (citing Va. S. Ct. R. 5:21, repealed and reinstated 
in substantially similar form as, Va. S.Ct. R. 5:17, 5:25). 


? The court in Bassette relied on Virginia Code section 
8.01-654 (B)(2). Bassette, 915 F.2d at 936. Section 654 (B)(2) states: 
“No writ shall be granted on the basis of any allegation the facts 
of which petitioner had knowledge at the time of filing any 


previous petition.” Va. Code Ann. § 8.01-654 (B)(2) (Michie 
1992). 


221 


In Slayton, the Virginia Supreme Court stated a simi- 
lar proposition: “[a] petition for a writ of habeas corpus 
may not be employed as a substitute for an appeal or a 
writ of error.” Id. at 682. The Supreme Court of the United | 
States addressed the same issue in Coleman v. Thompson, 
501 U.S. 722 (1991). The Court held that the doctrine of 
procedural default will bar a federal habeas petition ' 
when a prisoner fails to meet a state procedural require- 
ment. Id. at 750. Speaking for the Court, Justice O'Connor 
said: 


We now make it explicit: In all cases in 
which a state prisoner has defaulted his federal 
claims in state court pursuant to an independent 
and adequate state procedural rule, federal 
habeas review of the claims is barred unless the 
prisoner can demonstrate cause for the default 
and actual prejudice as a result of the alleged 


_ a oe 


violation of federal law, or demonstrate that 
failure to consider the claims will result in a 
fundamental miscarriage of justice. . .. We now 
recognize the important interest in finality 
served by state procedural rules, and the signifi- 
cant harm to the States that results from the 
failure of federal courts to respect them. 


Id. at 750. 


More recently, the issue has been addressed in Gray; 
Satcher v. Pruett, 126 F.2d 561 (1997); and Mu’Min, 125 
F.3d 192 (4th Cir. 1997). 


In Gray v. Netherland, 116 S.Ct. 2074 (1996), the Court, 
addressing a Brady claim, raised neither on direct appeal 
nor in the original state habeas corpus proceeding, stated: 


222 


[T]he procedural bar which gives rise to exhaus- 
tion provides an independent and adequate 
state-law ground for the conviction and sen- 
tence, and thus prevents federal habeas corpus 
review of the defaulted claim, unless the peti- 
tioner can demonstrate cause and prejudice for 
the default. 


In Virginia, “[n]o writ [of habeas corpus ad 
subjiciendum] shall be granted on the basis of 
any allegation that facts of which petitioner had 
knowledge at the time of filing any previous 
petition.” Va. Code Ann. § 8.01-654(B)(2) (1992). 
Because petitioner knew of the grounds of his 
Brady claim when he filed his first petition, 
§ 8.01-654(B)(2) precludes review of petitioner’s 
claim in any future state habeas proceeding. 
Because petitioner makes no attempt to demon- 
strate cause or prejudice for his default in state 
habeas proceedings, his claim is not cognizable 
in a federal suit for the writ. 


Id. at 2080-81 (citations omitted). 


In Mu’Min, petitioner sought to avoid the burden of 
procedural default by arguing that the rule in Slayton 
does not preclude federal consideration because it is not 
independent of federal law and that even if Slayton con- 
stitutes an adequate and independent ground for reject- 
ing his claims, they were, nevertheless, implicitly 
considered and rejected by the Supreme Court of Virginia 
during its mandatory review of his death sentence. Both 
arguments were rejected. 


The court noted that Slayton has been relied upon in 
the Fourth Circuit, e.g. Bennett v. Angelone, 92 F.3d 1336, 


223 


1343 (4th Cir. 1996) and is regularly cited in Virginia 
opinions. Mu’Min, 125 F.3d at 197. 


Moreover, the federal court is required to dismiss a 
procedurally defaulted claim absent a showing of justifia- 
ble cause resulting in actual prejudice. Wainwright v. 
Sykes, 433 U.S. 72 (1977). As the Fourth Circuit stated in 
Satcher, in order for a procedurally defaulted claim to be 
considered, petitioner “must demonstrate that the error 
worked to his ‘actual and substantial disadvantage,’ not 
merely that the error created a ‘possibility of prejudice.’ 
Id. at 572, citing Murry [sic] v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 478, 494 
(1986); Poyner v. Murray, 964 F.2d 1404, 1425 (4th Cir. 
1992). Petitioner has not made a showing of justifiable 
cause for his failure to appeal any of the defaulted issues 
to the Supreme Court of Virginia, either on direct appeal 
or in his original habeas petition. Therefore, the issue of 
actual prejudice need not be addressed. 


Respondent asserts that numerous claims by peti- 
tioner are procedurally defaulted. The Court concurs in 
some instances, but not in others, and accordingly, each 
of respondent's assertions will be dealt with individually. 


The Court finds that Claim 9 is procedurally 
defaulted, to the extent that it is founded upon refusal of 
the trial court to order production of a co-defendant’s 
prior inconsistent statement or upon the premise of pros- 
ecutorial misconduct in the denial of allegedly exculpa- 
tory material. No such issues were raised on direct 
appeal. Instead, all direct appellate claims were 
addressed in the context of trial court error (denial of 
polygraph information for impeachment purposes, denial 
of material regarding future dangerousness, and refusal 


dsaarindnene 


224 


to examine prosecutorial work product, in the form of 
witness statements, in camera). 


The claim of prosecutorial misconduct was first 
raised in the state habeas petition (Claim B(1)) and pre- 
sented the issues of nondisclosure of a prior inconsistent 
statement by a co-defendant, and the purported failure to 
reveal the Jocation at which a bullet was found or show 
trial counsel a particular photograph of the murder scene. 


Both state habeas claims were rejected — the claim of 
prosecutorial misconduct by application of Slayton, and 
the claim of trial court error by application of Hawks v. 
Cox, 175 S.E.2d 271 (Va. 1970) (“[a]bsent a change of 
circumstances, previous determination of the issues by 
either state or federal courts will be conclusive.” Id. at 
274. Any objections founded upon purported pros- 
ecutorial misconduct should have been made at the time 
of or during the course of the trial, and presented to the 
Virginia Supreme Court on direct appeal. The application 
of Slayton to these claims in the state habeas proceeding is 
consistent with prior judgments by the court. The present 
claim continues petitioner’s search for relief, based upon” 
polygraph test results for one of the co-defendants, and 
again raises the issue of the trial court's failure to review 
the prosecution’s file in camera. Polygraph material is not 
accessible under the Brady banner, when the results are 
inadmissible at trial, Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, 10 
(1995), and Virginia law prohibits use of polygraph 
results for impeachment purposes under any circum- 
stances. Robinson v. Commonwealth, 341 S.E.2d 159, 167 
(Va. 1986). 


225 


The portion of Claim 9 addressed to the refusal of the 
trial court to review the prosecution's file in camera to 
determine the presence of exculpatory material is 
addressed later in this opinion. 


Claim 13, the sufficiency of the Virginia Supreme 
Court proportionality review, was dismissed by the 
Supreme Court of Virginia on the basis of Slayton. How- 
ever, the issue of the appropriateness of the death pen- 
alty, and whether it was imposed under the influence of 
passion, prejudice, or any other arbitrary factor, or 
whether it was excessive or disproportionate to a penalty 
in similar cases was addressed on direct appeal; the sub- 
ject of the Supreme Court's original decision, Ramdass v. 
Commonwealth, 426 Va. 413, 426-27 (1993); and resubmit- 
ted in the state habeas corpus proceeding. Because 
Slayton addresses both the issue of objections not timely 
made and the use of a petition for writ of habeas corpus 
as a substitute for an appeal or writ of error, and because 
the Virginia Supreme Court was entitled to rely upon its 
own decision addressing the issue of whether the sen- 
tence was proportional and free of inflamed judgment by 
the jury, this Court finds that the issue has been appro- 
priately preserved and is not procedurally defaulted. 


Respondent alleges that Claim 1, the Simmons issue, 
has not been raised and is, therefore, procedurally 
defaulted. The Court disagrees. Regardless of the precise 
form or nature of the argument, there is one issue which 
has been preserved throughout this case, since it surfaced 
in the capital sentencing phase of the trial, and that is 
whether the sentencing jury was entitled to know peti- 
tioner’s parole eligibility status. The issue was presented 
on direct appeal, and ruled upon by the Virginia Supreme 


226 


Court. Ramdass, 246 Va. at 426. Certiorari was granted by 
the United States Supreme Court on the sole issue of 
whether the jury was entitled to know petitioner’s parole 
eligibility status when the Commonwealth relied upon 
the sole aggravating factor of future dangerousness. The 
matter was subsequently addressed by the Virginia 
Supreme Court and petitioner’s claim rejected on the sole 
ground that petitioner was parole ineligible because he 
had not received the requisite number of convictions. 
Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 450 S.E.2d 360 (Va. 1994). The 
claim was renewed in the state habeas proceeding as 
Claim C(13) and rejected under the rule in Hawks v. Cox, 
175 S.E.2d 271 (Va. 1970). The Court finds that this claim 
is not procedurally defaulted, and it will be addressed on 
the merits. 


Claim 2, denial of the constitutional right to the 
assistance of a mental health expert, is procedurally 
defaulted. No issue addressing denial of the “right to the 
assistance of a mental health expert” was presented on 
direct appeal. In the state habeas proceeding, trial coun- 
sel were accused of being ineffective for failing to secure 
a mental health expert who would assist in petitioner’s 
defense, (Claim A(4)), but a constitutional claim based on 
the purported failure to make such assistance available 
was not made. See Ake v. Oklahoma, 470 U.S. 68 (1985). The 
present claim constitutes an attack upon the alleged 
biases of the expert appointed by the court to assist in the 
preparation of such mental health issues as might be 
present. However, counsel for petitioner have not pre- 
sented the issue in any context fairly raised in either the 
trial court or the Virginia Supreme Court. Absent a show- 
ing of cause and prejudice for petitioner’s failure to assert 


227 


the claim in state court, he is not entitled to do so now. 
Slayton; Mu’Min v. Pruett, 125 F.3d 192, 196 (4th Cir. 1997). 
The claim is procedurally defaulted. 


Claim 3(a), denial of effective assistance of counsel 
because of an inadequate defense strategy, is pro- 
cedurally defaulted. The claim was not raised in the state 
habeas corpus action, the first occasion on which it could 
have been raised, and it may not now be presented for 
the first time. 


Likewise, Claim 3(b), alleging that petitioner was 
denied the effective assistance of counsel because of trial 
counsel’s advice to petitioner not to testify, is pro- 
cedurally defaulted. In the state habeas petition, habeas 
counsel alleged that trial counsel failed to put on evi- 
dence, (Claim A(20)), but in the course of presenting the 
claim stated only that trial counsel “met with petitioner 
and talked him out of testifying,” without alleging that 
such action constituted ineffective assistance of counsel. 
This Court is not required to assume that the language 
state habeas counsel selected was to be viewed in the 
pejorative sense. The current claim asserts that trial coun- 
sel were negligent, and that they “unreasonably recom- 
mended that [petitioner] not testify.” (Pet. at { 158.) That 
is a marked departure from the state habeas claim and is, 
therefore, procedurally defaulted. Mu’Min; Duncan v. 
Henry, 513 U.S. 364, 366 (1995) (per curiam). 


Respondent asserts that Claim 3(f), ineffective assis- 
tance of counsel by virtue of trial counsel’s failure to 
obtain experts to assist in the defense of the case and 
rebut prosecution experts, is procedurally defaulted as to 
claims pertaining to the location of the bullet. The Court 


228 


agrees. However, the other claims were fairly raised in 
the state habeas corpus petition and rejected by the Vir- 
ginia Supreme Court on the merits. The issues have been 
preserved for appeal. 


Respondent asserts that Claim 5, ineffective assis- 
tance of counsel for failing to establish parole ineligibility 
by failing to request rescheduling of the trial and by 
failure to challenge the determination that petitioner was 
not eligible for parole, are procedurally defaulted. The 
Court agrees, as to the former issue, but disagrees as to 
the latter. Neither issue was raised in the state habeas 
proceeding in the context of ineffective assistance of 
counsel, even though at the time the state habeas petition 
was filed, the case had been remanded to the Virginia 
Supreme Court by the United States Supreme Court and 
dismissed on a supplemental opinion, Ramdass v. Com- 
monwealth, 248 Va. 518 (1994), on the grounds that peti- 
tioner was not eligible for parole. Thus, both of the issues 
raised in the current proceeding were known to state 
habeas counsel, and their failure to raise the ineffective 
assistance of counsel claim results in the claim being 
procedurally defaulted. However, the Simmons issue was 
expressly preserved. 


Respondent asserts that Claim 6(a), alleging ineffec- 
tive assistance of counsel via inadequate voir dire prior to 
empaneling of the jury, was not raised. However, the 
Court finds that matter was addressed in the state habeas 
petition, Claim A(2), and dismissed on the merits. Where 
the claim is that counsel was ineffective because of an 
inadequate voir dire examination, the fact that the tenor of 
the argument may change does not render the matter 
procedurally defaulted. Current counsel are simply not 


229 


bound to adhere to the arguments of state habeas counsel 
which have already been rejected by one tribunal. The 
state was put on notice, the issue was preserved, and the 
claim is not procedurally defaulted and will be addressed 
on the merits. 


Respondent asserts that Claim 10, addressing the 
issue of the constitutionality of Virginia’s death penalty 
sentencing statutes, has not been raised, but the Court 
finds otherwise. The issues were raised on direct appeal 
in the context of the adequacy of the proportionality 
review in the state habeas corpus action, and the Eighth 
and Fourteenth Amendment issues arising out of the 
Virginia death penalty have been properly preserved and 
will be considered on the merits. 


Finally, respondent alleges that Claim 12, the admis- 
sion of unadjudicated misconduct in the penalty phase in 
violation of petitioner’s constitutional rights, was not 
raised in the state court. However, the matter was raised 
on direct appeal and addressed in the first Virginia 
Supreme Court opinion, Ramdass, 246 Va. at 418; raised in 
the state habeas petition and dismissed under the rule in 
Hawks, (Claim C(12)); and preserved for consideration by 
this Court. The claim is not procedurally defaulted. 


The remainder of the report and recommendation 
will be addressed to the claims which are not pro- 
cedurally defaulted. 


230 


E. Claims Addressed on the Merits. 
1. General 


Judge Wilkinson, in the course of addressing 
numerous purported constitutional claims in a death pen- 
alty case said: “It is becoming all too commonplace to 
charge even diligent counsel in the midst of difficult 
circumstances with the adverse outcome in a capital 
case.” Bunch v. Thompson, 949 F.2d 1354, 1363 (4th Cir. 
1991). The case involved a petitioner who shot his girl- 
friend in cold blood; ransacked her house and stole 
expensive jewelry; and before leaving, knotted a scarf 
around the decedent’s neck and pulled her unconscious 
body to a door where he hung her by the scarf from the 
door knob. Id. at 1358. The autopsy established that her 
death was a combination of the gunshot wound to the 
head and suffocation as a result of the hanging. Id. The 
complaints in that case ran the gamut: ineffective assis- 
tance of counsel, instructional errors, and a biased jury. 
The tenor of the comment could be modified to state that 
it has become commonplace, in every capital case, to 
assume that some error of constitutional dimension has 
lead [sic] to a decision that the defendant is guilty of the 
capital offense and that the conduct and background of 
the guilty party justifies the death penalty. Judge Wilkin- 
son, and other jurists called upon to deal with death 
penalty cases, find their experience to be otherwise. 


It is, therefore, appropriate to again turn to the lan- 
guage of Judge Wilkinson in Sherman v. Smith, 89 F.3d 
1134 (4th Cir. 1996), where he stated: 

Criminal defendants in this country are entitled 

to a fair, but not a perfect trial. “[G]iven the 


231 


myriad safeguards provided to assure a fair 
trial, and taking into account the reality of the 
human fallibility of the participants, there can 
be no such thing as an error-free, perfect trial,” 
United States v. Hasting, 461 U.S. 499, 508 (1983) 
and the Constitution does not demand one. This 
focus on fairness, rather than on perfection, pro- 
tects society from individuals who have been 
duly and fairly convicted of crimes, thereby pro- 
moting “public respect for the criminal process.” 
De Lawrence v. Van Arsdale, 475 U.S. 673, 681 
(1986). 


Sherman at 1137. 


The statement is made in the context of the language 
of Arizona v. Fulminante, 499 U.S. 279 (1991), in which the 
Supreme Court noted “that a constitutional error does not 
automatically require reversal of a conviction,” noting 
that “the Court has applied harmless-error analysis to a 
wide range of errors and has recognized that most consti- 
tutional errors can be harmless.”* Id. at 306. The more 
difficult issue has always been what standard to apply in 
determining whether an error is harmful. Judge Wilkin- 
son addressed the error in Sherman, using the phrase 
“structural errors” to describe errors so severe as to ren- 
der a trial inherently unfair and thus, not subject to a 
harmless error analysis, the only correct analysis to be 
applied to the claims now before the Court. 


3 Both the Supreme Court in Fulminante, 499 U.S. at 306-07, 
and Judge Wilkinson in Sherman, 89 F.3d at 1137, catalogued 
errors deemed to be constitutional in scope but harmless in the 
effect on the outcome of trial. 


232 


In Brecht v. Abrahamson, 507 U.S. 619 (1993), the 
Supreme Court had to choose between competing stan- 
dards for harmless error analysis. In conflict was the 
standard articulated in Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 
(1967), necessitating a finding that an error “was harm- 
less beyond a reasonable doubt,” id. at 24, juxtaposed 
against Kotteakos v. United States, 328 U.S. 750 (1946), in 
which the Court required that for habeas relief to be 
granted, the error must have “had substantial and inju- 
rious effect or influence in determining the jury’s ver- 
dict.” Id. at 776. 


The plurality decision, adopting the Kotteakos stan- 
dard by a five to four vote, but yielding six separate 
opinions, four in dissent, is the standard by which this 
Court is governed. The opinion, and the standard which 
is adopted, is merely a reflection of the principle that 
collateral review is different from direct review, id. at 633, 
and that direct review is the principal avenue for chal- 
lenging a conviction. Id. The Brecht standard was applied 
in Sherman and is applicable to the case now before the 
Court. 


2. Petitioner did not receive ineffective assis- 
tance of counsel. 


a. Applicable standard 


Probably the only easy aspect of the matter before the 
Court is articulating the standards upon which claims of 
ineffective assistance of counsel are to be judged. The 
standards are established in Strickland v. Washington, 466 
U.S. 668 (1984), and have been applied on numerous 
occasions by this Court. 


233 


In Strickland, the Supreme Court approved as the 
proper standard for attorney performance that of “rea- 
sonably effective assistance”. Id. at 687. The Court stated: 


The benchmark for judging any claim of ineffec- 
tiveness must be whether counsel’s conduct so 
undermined the proper functioning of the 
adversarial process that the trial cannot be 
relied on as having produced a just result. 


A convicted defendant's claim that coun- 
sel’s assistance was so defective as to require 
reversal of a conviction or death sentence has 
two components. First, the defendant must 
show that counsel’s performance was deficient. 
This requires showing that counsel made errors 
so serious that counsel was not functioning as 
the “counsel” guaranteed the defendant by the 
Sixth Amendment. Second, the defendant must 
show that the deficient performance prejudiced 
the defense. This requires showing that coun- 
sel’s errors were so serious as to deprive the 
defendant of a fair trial, a trial whose result is 
reliable. Unless a defendant makes both show- 
ings, it cannot be said that the conviction or 
death sentence resulted from a breakdown in 
the advérsary process that renders the result 
unreliable. 


Id. at 686-87. 


There are, therefore, two elements of the Strickland 
test, each of which must be met before the conduct of 
counsel can be found to be constitutionally defective. 
First, petitioner must show that he received deficient 
legal representation, measuring the competency of his 
counsel against what “an objectively reasonable attorney 


234 


would have done under the circumstances existing at the 
time of the representation,” Savino v. Murray, 82 F.3d 593, 
599 (4th Cir. 1996), and petitioner must show actual preju- 
dice, that is, he “must demonstrate that the error worked 
to his ‘actual and substantial disadvantage,’ not merely 
that the error created a ‘possibility of prejudice.’ ” Satcher 
v. Pruett, 126 F.3d 561, 572 (4th Cir. 1997) (quoting Murray 
v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 478, 494 (1986); Poyner v. Murray, 964 
F.2d 1404, 1425 (4th Cir. 1992)). 


In analyzing trial counsel's performance under the 
“deficiency” prong of Strickland, a reviewing court is 
required to be highly deferential in scrutinizing counsel's 
tactics. Bennett v. Angelone, 92 F.3d 1336, 1349 (4th Cir. 
1996) (citing Strickland, 466 U.S. at 689). Further, review- 
ing courts are admonished to “not permit the benefit of 
hindsight to impact. . . review,” Bell v. Evatt, 72 F.3d 421, 
429 (4th Cir. 1995) (citing Strickland, 466 U.S. at 689). 


Respondent argues that in reviewing petitioner's 
ineffective counsel claims “this Court not only is limited 
to the facts as they were presented to the Virginia 
Supreme Court, but also is limited to determination of 
whether the state court reasonably rejected the claim 
under Strickland v. Washington.” (Resp. Mem. at 16 (cita- 
tion omitted).) The Court does not agree. 


First, “[w]hether counsel's performance was constitu- 
tionally adequate is a mixed question of law and 
fact ...,” and on appeal is reviewed de novo. Savino, 82 
F.3d at 598; Stout v. Netherland, No. 95-4008, 4007 1996 WL 
496601 (4th Cir. September 3, 1996); see also Ostrander v. 
Green, 46 F.3d 347, 354 (4th Cir. 1995); Poyner, 964 F.2d at 
1416. 


235 


Second, this Court is not relegated to the status of a 
judicial rubber stamp on any issues that may have “had 
substantial and injurious effect or influence in determin- 
ing the jury’s verdict.” Brecht at 637; Kotteakos at 776. 
Inadequate representation, when it occurs, frequently has 
a “substantial and injurious effect.” 


Before addressing the individual claims of ineffective 
assistance, it is appropriate to consider, at the threshold, 
the level of effort extended on petitioner’s behalf by trial 
counsel. 


The record reveals that trial counsel filed a total of 
more than thirty motions on petitioner’s behalf, address- 
ing matters of evidence, transcripts, the size of the jury 
panel, a proposed jury survey, appointment of a mental 
health expert, discovery, a request for additional attor- 
neys, exculpatory evidence, identification of experts, sen- 
tencing, the constitutionality of the death penalty, and 
other matters relevant to petitioner’s case. There were 
motion hearings on September 25, 1992; November 2, 
1992; December 8, 1992; and January 8, 1993. 


Counsel was given great latitude by the trial court 
during voir dire and extensively questioned members of 
the jury, successfully challenging a number of panel 
members. However, as might be expected, not all of coun- 
sel’s challenges were upheld. That there were some panel 
members unacceptable to the defense is neither unusual 
nor unconstitutional. 


Trial counsel were faced with two eye-witness co- 
defendants, the recovery of the gun used in the killing, 
and medical evidence that the decedent was shot at 
point-blank range, all in the guilt phase of the trial. 


236 


During the sentencing phase of the trial, counsel were 
faced with the testimony of a victim shot and wounded 
only a week before the murder of Kayani, the testimony 
of a brother who was in prison at the same time as 
petitioner, evidence of an extensive criminal history for 
such a young man, and a mother able to provide only 
minimal assistance. 


Trial counsel had terrible facts to work with, an unso- 
phisticated client with a history of escalating propensities 
for violence, and difficult choices at every turn. 


Finally, it should not be forgotten that trial counsel 
were successful in obtaining a writ of certiorari in the 
United States Supreme Court and preserving a significant 
issue arising out of the Supreme Court's decision in Sim- 
mons v. South Carolina, 512 U.S. 154 (1994). 


It is a given that with the perfect clarity of hindsight, 
some things might be done in a different manner if coun- 
sel were given a second opportunity to try the case. The 
Court is, however, bound by the record. 


b. The issue of “trial strategy” is irrele- 
vant. 


As the Fourth Circuit has noted, a lawyer’s perfor- 
mance is entitled to a strong presumption of reasonable- 
ness. Evans v. Thompson, 881 F.2d 117, 124 (4th Cir. 1989); 
Pruett, 996 F.2d at 1568. Strickland advises courts to apply 
a “heavy measure of deference to counsel’s judgments,” 
Strickland at 691, a sentiment embraced by the Fourth 
Circuit in Satcher and reflected in a series of earlier opin- 
ions from this Court. See also Stout, 1996 W.L. 496601 at *7 


237 


(counseling that courts “resist [] the temptation to sec- 
ond-guess the adequacy of counsel’s strategy based 
solely on the fact that the strategy has resulted in an 
adverse outcome”); Bell v. Evatt, 72 F.3d 421, 429 (4th Cir. 
1995) (“We emphasize . . . that neither Bell nor any other 
aggrieved defendant can manipulate this forum to con- 
strue a reasonable but ultimately unsuccessful strategy in 
his favor. Standing alone, unsuccessful trial tactics nei- 
ther constitute prejudice nor definitely prove ineffective 
assistance of counsel”); Bennett, 92 F.3d at 1349 (finding 
that rational, if imperfect, trial strategy is not ineffective- 
ness); Bunch, 949 F.2d at 1363 (“When examining ineffec- 
tive assistance claims, however, we must appreciate the 
practical limitations and tactical decisions that trial coun- 
sel faced.”) 


1. Trial counsel were not ineffective 
because they advised petitioner not 
to testify. 


It is difficult to imagine a decision more personal to, 
and more dependant upon, the relationship between an 
attorney and his or her client than the decision address- 
ing whether his or her client should testify. 


The affidavits in this case make clear that trial coun- 
sel were struggling with a difficult factual situation, 
knowing before trial that the Commonwealth had the 
benefit of the testimony of two co-defendants and that 
petitioner had an extraordinarily had record which could 
be used against him in the guilt phase. The affidavits also 
reveal that the decision not to have petitioner testify was 
made at the last minute but specifically after consultation 


238 


between petitioner and lead trial counsel. The decision 
was collaborative rather than unilateral. , 


The decision not to have petitioner testify at trial 
represented an informed change of strategy in the midst 
of trial. Such a decision is “virtually unchallengeable” on 
appeal. Turner v. Williams, 35 F.3d 872, 904 (4th Cir. 1994) 
(quoting Strickland, 466 U.S. at 690). See also Williams v. 
Kelly, 816 F.2d 939, 947 (4th Cir. 1987) (advice to robbery 
defendant to testify found not to be ineffective assis- 
tance); Jones v. Murray, 947 F.2d 1106 (4th Cir. 1991) (not 
ineffective assistance for trial counsel to neither recom- 
mend nor attempt to persuade defendant fo accept a plea 
bargain); Clozza v. Murray, 913 F.2d 1092, 1100-01 (4th Cir. 
1990) (not ineffective assistance of counsel to concede 
guilt). 


2. Trial counsel was not ineffective for 
failing to confront the prosecution’s 
argument that the gun was fired 
twice and in an upward position. 


This is merely an extension of petitioner’s argument 
that his trial counsel were ineffective for failing to have 
him testify. As appropriately noted by respondent, peti- 
tioner had already entered a plea of guilty to the robbery. 
Two eye-witnesses had already testified that petitioner 
had a gun; one of the eye-witnesses heard petitioner 
threaten the clerk, and the other saw petitioner squat 
down next to the clerk, place the gun to his head, and 
shoot him, after which he stood up laughing. Two of 
petitioner’s co-defendants had already testified against 
him. Petitioner had a prior felony record, which had not 


239 


yet been brought out. There were numerous reasons why 
it might be appropriate for him not to take the witness 
stand. After consultation with his counsel, petitioner 
elected not [sic] take the witness stand. 


Counsels’ advice not to testify is a paradigm of the 
type of tactical decision that cannot be challenged as 
evidence of ineffective assistance. Hutchens v. Garrison, 
724 F.2d 1425, 1436 (4th Cir. 1983) 


3. Trial counsel did not fail to challenge 
the testimony of the Common- 
wealth’s medical examiner, Dr. 
Francis Fields. 


This claim has several components. First, habeas 
counsel object to the fact that Fields was not subjected to 
any questioning concerning her qualifications; second, 
the handling of her testimony is attacked on the grounds 
that she was not “qualified to testify as to projectile 
direction, trajectory or shooting reconstruction,” (Pet. 
{ 165); third, there is an attack on trial counsel because 
Fields testified without objection from the defense that 
the bullet was fired in a forward, upward and rightward 
direction; and finally, trial counsel are criticized for ven- 
turing into the subject of powder burns around the 
wound. (Pet. {4 165-70.) All of the objections constitute 
the quintessential type of “Monday morning quarter- 
backing” which Strickland precludes. 


Examination of the trial transcript reveals that Fields’ 
testimony was limited to the scope of her professional 
knowledge, as a medical examiner. The fact that her 


240 


qualifications as a medical examiner were stipulated to is 
“virtually unchallengeable.” Turner; Strickland. 


Fields’ testimony was identical to the facts set forth 
in the autopsy report, a copy of which was furnished to 
counsel well before trial. Her testimony of the path that 
the bullet took from the time that it entered the victim's 
body until the time that it exited the victim’s body was 
based upon her first-hand knowledge, acquired in the 
course of performing the autopsy on the victim, and was 
clearly within her area of expertise and range of compe- 
tence insofar as the opinion reflected in that testimony is 
concerned. 


Fields answered only the questions which were 
addressed to her. She did not testify about the angle at 
which the gun was held, only the path the bullet took. 
Photographic exhibits confirmed her testimony. Any con- 
clusions about petitioner’s conduct were left to the jury. 


Finally, defense counsel was entitled to explore the 
subject of powder burns. That the line of questioning was 
unhelpful does not mean that counsel was constitu- 
tionally ineffective. 


The claim is without merit. 


4. Trial counsel did not fail to ade- 
quately cross-examine the “firearms 
identification” expert. 


The gravamen of this complaint is that trial counsel 
permitted Julian Mason to testify in areas beyond that of 
“firearms identification,” the expertise ascribed to him in 
the claim. However, the claim misrepresents the record. 


241 


Trial counsel filed a motion to compel the state to 
identify any expert witnesses they intended to call. (Rec. 
at 0096.) At a hearing on January 8, 1993, the motion was 
specifically addressed. Trial counsel acknowledged that 
the medical expert would be Doctor Fields, and the 
autopsy report was a part of the record to which trial 
counsel had complete access. The Commonwealth stated 
“the only other expert, other than the medical examiner, 
that I am aware of that has done anything in connection 
with this case would be Julian Mason, the ballistics man 
from the Division of Forensic Science.” (1/8/93 Tr. at 4.) 
The Commonwealth went on to say that the defense had 
had the ballistics report since November 28, 1992. The 
Commonwealth Attorney noted that the bullet which was 
recovered was so mangled, it could not be used to prove 
anything. However, he went on to represent to the 
defense the following: 


[A]nd the only other thing he would say is that 
this .38 caliber revolver is capable of firing 9 
millimeter rounds. 


And it would cause the same kind of micro- 
scopic markings on the bullets as the bullet he 
found on the floor. But he can’t say it came from 
that gun. 


The only reason he would testify is to testify 
that 9 millimeters will fire in a .38 caliber 
revolver. 


THE COURT: My question is, Mr. Arrif, does 
that answer your question? 


MR. ARRIF: Yes, it does. We'll withdraw that 
motion, Judge. 


(1/8/93 Tr. at 5.) 


242 


The testimony of Mason at trial strictly followed the 
representations of the attorney for the Commonwealth. 


The only testimony which went beyond that 
described by the Commonwealth during the motion hear- 
ing was the testimony of Mason that there was no evi- 
dence that the gun would “misfire,” nor was there any 
evidence to suggest that the gun had a “hair trigger.” 
Both of these subjects were properly raised on redirect 
examination, after the subject of the mechanical condition 
of the murder weapon was properly raised by trial coun- 
sel on cross-examination. 


While the defense cross-examination of the expert 
may reflect the consequences of violating the old legal 
maxim, “if you don’t know the answer, don’t ask the 
question,” there is not a single question propounded to 
the expert, whether on direct or cross, that was reason- 
ably outside of the expected range of testimony of Mason, 
given his previous identification by the Commonwealth 
as a “ballistics expert.” Further, his ability to identify the 
operating mechanism of a firearm is totally consistent 
with his identification as a “firearms identification” 
expert. 


This claim is without merit. 


5. Trial counsel were not ineffective for 
failing to seek outside assistance. 


This claim is precisely what Judge Wilkinson referred 
to when he wrote “[i]t is becoming all too commonplace 
to charge even diligent counsel in the midst of difficult 


243 


circumstances with the adverse outcome in a capital 
case.” Bunch at 1363. 


The claim is presented through a number of different 
attacks on the efforts of trial counsel. There is criticism of 
the fact that trial counsel essentially conceded that peti- 
tioner fired the fatal shot; that trial counsel was at fault 
for failing to acquire a defense expert to testify “that the 
angle of the bullet was probably in either a downward 

. or sideways position,” (Pet. { 188), assuming such 
proposed testimony would have been permitted; that trial 
counsel was ineffective for objecting to Exhibit 14, a 
photograph showing where a bullet was recovered, (Pet. 
{ 190); the failure of counsel to object to the testimony of 
a police officer who identified the photograph and the 
bullet, and additionally, identified what was thought to 
have been a ricochet, (Pet. { 194); and the failure to have 
a witness testify that certain clicking sounds heard by 
witnesses as petitioner exited the 7-11 came from the 
door opener, as opposed to the gun petitioner was hold- 
ing, (Pet. ¥ 197). 


It first need be said that counsel have offered nothing 
to establish that any of the theories which are discussed 
in the course of this objection are plausible, let alone 
capable of being supported by a credible witness. Second, 
the photograph to which counsel objected showed a bul- 
let in the area of the vicinity where the decedent was 
shot. The basis for the objection was that the Common- 
wealth failed to lay a foundation to establish that that 
was the bullet that had killed the decedent and second, 
that counsel had not previously been shown the location 
where the bullet was recovered. Neither of the objections 
were valid. The police officer who photographed the 


244 


crime scene identified a photograph showing the bullet 
on the floor of the 7-11 and then identified the bullet. The 
second part of the objection was blunted by trial coun- 
sel’s acknowledgment that he may have seen the bullet in 
another picture shown to him by the Commonwealth 
prior to trial. (1/27/93 Tr. at 66-70, 73.) Finally, the testi- 
mony of the police officer that he believed the bullet 
ricocheted off of the safe was admitted, without objec- 
tion. Assuming, arguendo, that trial counsel should have 
objected to the testimony, the failure to do so was harm- 
less. It could not possibly have damaged the defense’s 
case, given the unrebutted testimony that petitioner fired 
the gun while squatting down beside the decedent next to 
the safe off which the bullet is alleged to have ricocheted. 
Any error which may have occurred fails to rise to a 
constitutional level. 


Prior to Strickland, the Fourth Circuit recognized that 
the standard for attorney conduct “does not require that 
representation be flawless, only that all decisions mate- 
rially affecting defendant's representation be the product 
of informed judgment, not neglect or ignorance.” Sallie v. 
North Carolina, 587 F.2d 636, 640 (4th Cir. 1978). 


The standard articulated in Strickland makes even 
clearer that the conduct of counsel must result in actual 


prejudice to a defendant before it rises to a constitutional 
level. 


This claim does not rise to the Strickland standard. 


245 


c. Trial counsel was not ineffective for fail- 
ing to object to co-defendant’s testimony 
about his sentence for the murders. 


This particular claim is not addressed in any of the 
affidavits of trial counsel. Thus, habeas counsel are left to 
speculate, in the form of a claim, about the failure to 
object. 

It-is easy to see from the presentation of petitioner's 
counsel, and the respondent’s answer, that reasonable 
counsel could differ. Petitioner, by counsel, says that the 
presentation of a co-defendant’s life sentence had the 
affect [sic] of leaving the jury “to impose a harsher sen- 
tence on Mr. Ramdass to reflect his comparative cul- 
pability rather than because the sentence was appropriate 
on its own.” (Pet. J 202.) 


Responder replies by citing Coppola v. Common- 
wealth, 257 S.E.2d 797 (Va. 1979), in which the defense 
sought to offer in evidence the fact that a co-defendant in 
a brutal killing had been convicted of capital murder and 
sentenced to life, but the evidence was excluded on the 
grounds that it did not constitute mitigation evidence 
under section 19.2-264.4 of the Virginia Code and that the 
trial court did not abuse its discretion in refusing it as 
irrelevant. Copolla at 804-05. 


Singh participated in the planning and execution of 
the robbery resulting in Kayani’s death. He was as culpa-
