ble and susceptible to capital punishment as petitioner. It 
was not ineffective assistance to permit the jury to know 
that Singh would live. It was appropriate to make the 
jury aware that a co-defendant, testifying against peti- 
tioner, would live on. 


246 


It is precisely this kind of claim which gives rise to 
the admonition that reviewing courts must be “highly 
deferential” in scrutinizing trial counsel’s tactics. Strick- 
land, 466 U.S. at 689, applied in Beavers v. Pruett, No. 9704, 
1997 WL 585739 (4th Cir., September 23, 1997); Bennett v. 
Angelone, 92 F.3d 1336, 1349 (4th Cir. 1996). 


d. Miscellaneous claims of ineffectiveness. 


1. Trial counsel did not conduct ade- 
quate voir dire (Claim 6(a)). 


This claim incorporates several allegations, not all of 
which will be specifically addressed. 


Habeas counsel addresses the makeup of the jury (all 
white), the background of the jury (several had read 
accounts in the media), and the experience of the jury 
(some members of the panel had been victims of violent 
crimes). 


Counsel particularly objects to the retention of jurors 
Powell and Ross, who, in response to very confusing 
questions, appeared to have difficulty with the concept of 
considering various mitigating circumstances. 


Counsel object to members of the panel who 
described the crime as “brutal” (Juror Owens, 5/18/93 Tr. 
at 47), “horrible” (Juror Peoples, 5/18/93 Tr. at 50), a 
juror who was a friend of a witness in the case, (id. at 43), 
two jurors whose children or in-laws were police officers 
in Fairfax County, (id. at 68-69), several jurors familiar 
with the crime scene, (id. at 45-47), and eight jurors that 
were victims of crimes, (id. at 83-88). Finally, counsel 
object to the retention on the panel of Juror Amelia 


247 


because she indicated she might have “reservations” if 
petitioner didn’t testify. (Id. at 90.) 


On direct appeal, trial counsel presented three issues: 
that the trial court erred in refusing to dismiss a juror 
personally acquainted with one of the witnesses and a 
second juror who had preconceived notions about the 
brutality of the crime (Assignment of Error 18), that the 
trial court erred in refusing to permit inquiry into 
whether jurors had the belief that petitioner would be 
executed if the death sentence was imposed (Assignment 
of Error 19), and that the trial court erred in denying the 
opportunity to voir dire the jury regarding specific mitiga- 
tion circumstances (Assignment of Error 20). All of these 
claims were abandoned on appeal and treated by the 
court as waived. Ramdass, 246 Va. at 419. In the state 
habeas petition, only three claims were raised: trial coun- 
sel’s failure to move to strike Amelia for cause, the failure 
of trial counsel to move for individual voir dire, and the 
failure to question the majority of the panel regarding 
consideration of mitigating factors and sentencing. All of 
these issues were rejected by the Supreme Court on the 
merits. 


The only issues which survive for consideration are 
those pertaining to jurors Amelia, Ross, and Powell. The 
latter two jurors were the last jurors questioned about 
their willingness to consider mitigating factors. 


The parties agreed “to have a panel of *venty-three 
[jurors] . . . and that we agree that both sides will have 
five preemptory challenges, which will then leave one 
juror as an alternate in case there is a need for it.” 
(1/26/93 Tr. at 27.) 


248 


Examination of the transcript reveals that the ques- 
tioning of Ross and Powell occupies twenty pages of 
record. In the course of the examination, it is clear that 
the defense counsel asked inarticulate questions on occa- 
sion which evoked responses from the witnesses indicat- 
ing that certain elements of the case could not be 
considered by them in mitigation, although each of the 
jurors acknowledged that they could follow the instruc- 
tions of the court if told to consider certain mitigating 
factors. At the conclusion of the examination, trial coun- 
sel objected to each of the jurors and moved to strike 
them for cause stating “[yJour honor they have demon- 
strated that they will not consider specific facts as mit- 
igating factors.” (5/18/93 Tr. at 278.) In fact, counsel 
emphasized that the responses to the questions by the 
jurors indicated that they could not consider things such 
as age in mitigation of the sentence, the matter about 
which counsel was most concerned 


As to Ross and Powell, there is no question that their 
presence on the jury was challenged, that the challenges 
were overruled, and that the decision of the trial court 
was within the sound discretion of the trial court to 
make. This element of the claim is without merit. 


The claim with regard to juror Amelia has even less 
merit. In the course of general questions posed to the 
panel, Amelia said she thought she might have reserva- 
tions if petitioner didn’t testify. However, the transcript, 
thereafter, fails to reveal that Amelia was ever selected as 
a member of the trial panel. 


The transcript for January 26, 1993, reveals that the 
trial panel consisted of jurors Matthys, Oglesby, Tkach, 


249 


Wood, May, Owens, Volpe, McClellan, Padberg, Schues- 
sler, Young, Patterson, McMahon, McDaniel, Peoples, 
Shavlik, McEntire, Sillex, McNette, Noud, Moreau, Pow- 
ell, and Ross. Amelia was not a part of the panel. 
(1/26/93 Tr. at 285-89.) 


Powell, Moreau, Peoples, McMann, Patterson, Young, 
Owens, Wood, Oglesby, and Mathis were struck. Powell 
was the fifth preemptory strike by the defense. Ross was 
the alternate and was excused prior to the commence- 
ment of deliberations (1/28/93 Tr. at 63.) 


Petitioner does not assert claims against any of the 
remaining members of the jury panel. Since all of the 
members of the panel to whom petitioner objected were 
removed from the panel, prior to deliberations, no claim 
based on a lack of jury impartiality can survive. Ross v. 
Oklahoma, 487 U.S. 81, 85-86 (1987). All of the remaining 
jurors were constitutionally impaneled, and this claim is 
without merit. 


2. Counsel was not ineffective for con- 
ceding the nature of petitioner’s 
background (Claim 6(b)). 


This claim hinges upon counsel’s statement that 
“Bobby Lee Ramdass was a bad boy. He’s a bad man. 
He’s committed crimes of violence in the past. He won't 
stand up here and tell you that he won’t commit crimes of 
violence in the future,” made in the defense opening 
statement during the sentencing phase. (Pet. { 233; 
1/29/93 Tr. at 98.) 


250 » 


The next paragraph of the petition effectively 
acknowledges that the statement was a reflection of trial 
strategy: “counsel may have hoped in this way to estab- 
lish credibility for their mitigation theory. ... ” (Pet. 
{ 234.) Strickland precludes us from second-guessing. 


Further, statements which may appear to concede an 
element of the prosecution’s case, or present a defendant 
in a bad light, are consistent with trial strategy of seeking 
to maintain credibility with the jury and do not constitute 
ineffective assistance of counsel. Clozza v. Murray, 913 
F.2d 1092, 1098 (4th Cir. 1990); Bell v. Evatt, 72 F.3d 421 
(4th Cir. 1995); Brown v. Dixon, 891 F.2d 490, 500 n.20 (4th 
Cir. 1989). 


As the court noted in Beli, “unsuccessful trial tactics 
neither constitute prejudice nor definitively prove inef- 
fective assistance of counsel.” Id. at 429. 


3. Trial counsel were not ineffective in 
their preparation for the sentencing 
phase of trial (Claim 6(c)). 


Habeas counsel make two arguments, into which 
they attempt to blend the procedurally defaulted claims 
concerning Dr. Samenow, the mental health expert 
appointed by the court. 


Because the issue of Dr. Samenow is procedurally 
defaulted, his assistance, or lack thereof, will not be fur- 
ther addressed. 


Counsel claim that the jury was denied mental health 
information prepared in the course of prior evaluations 
as reflected in petitioner’s juvenile files and that the 


251 


evaluations would provide strong mitigating evidence. 
(Pet. | 239.) The petition states that the defense 
attempted to present the information through the hearsay 
testimony of a probation officer, which was objected to by 
the prosecution and barred by the court. Id. That is not a 
correct statement. 


The prosecution objected to certain testimony offered 
by the defense through Jennifer Joffe, a probation and 
parole officer for the Commonwealth of Virginia who had 
conducted a presence investigation on petitioner four 
years earlier. (1/29/93 Tr. at 115.) Joffe was asked about 
petitioner's mother, and more particularly about Mrs. 
Ramdass’s physical health history. The Commonwealth 
objected. After some discussion, and a determination that 
Mrs. Ramdass would testify, the Commonwealth’s objec- 
tion was sustained. (Id. at 120-21.) However, Joffe was 
permitted to testify, and did testify about petitioner’s 
counseling. The record reveals that she testified peti- 
tioner was in counseling and psychotherapy from August 
through September, 1986, and October through Novem- 
ber, 1986, and for a short time when he was on parole, 
and that therapy was terminated “because he saw no 
reason to be in therapy. Another time, it was terminated 
because he was recommitted to the state department of 
correct[io]ns.” (1/29/93 Tr. at 123.) The real problem for 
petitioner came on cross-examination when the witness 
was asked whether the reports indicated that petitioner 
was psychotic or suffering from severe mental problems. 
The answer to each question was no. (1/29/93 Tr. at 
126-27.) 


Habeas counsel also argue that the mitigation phase 
was constitutionally handicapped by the nonappearance 


ee ee a 


Whe hee. OR -- 


252 


of a guidance counselor. Apparently, trial counsel 
thought that the witness had been recognized, but she 
had not. Upon discovering that fact, trial counsel notified 
the court, and the record contains a proffer of her 
expected testimony: 


Your Honor, Ms. Mayo, her full name is June 
Mayo, is a seventh grade teacher at the Carl 
Sandberg Middle School in Alexandria, Virginia. 
She was Bobby’s seventh grade teacher. She’s 


one of the few people that Bobby would actually 
talk to. 


He would tell her about his life and such. 
She told me he did fairly well in her classes, not 
anywhere else and that she liked him and that 
she was aware that he did have problems else- 
where in school and with his family. 


That’s pretty much it. 
(1/29/93 Tr. at 242.) 


That proffer was permitted to go to the jury without 
objection by the Commonwealth. Thus, the essence of the 
testimony that Mayo would have offered was not lost to 


petitioner but made known to the jury in the form of a 
proffer. 


Finally, in addition to Joffe, trial counsel called to the 
witness stand the petitioner, his brother, and his mother. 
As a prelude to his mitigation case, trial counsel made an 
extensive opening statement and informed the jury of the 
myriad problems experienced by petitioner, describing 
his family as “unbelievably dysfunctional” and referring 
to petitioner as one of “Fagan’s children from Dicken’s 
[sic] London.” (1/29/93 Tr. at 91) He described peti- 
tioner’s lack of school experience; his experimentation 


253 


with PCP, cocaine, marijuana, and alcohol; his prior incar- 
cerations for serious offenses; his pending charges in 
other jurisdictions; his minimal contact with his father; 
his residence in a storage shed of a condominium for a 
while; his abject poverty; his lack of any serious parent- 
ing; and his total absence of any employment history. 


Reasonable attorneys might have argued about how 
to present this background, but it is unlikely that there 
was much additional material which could have been 
offered in mitigation given the facts which the jury heard 
in the guilt phase and the criminal history which the jury 
heard in the sentencing phase. The decisions made by his 
counsel in presenting the sentencing portion of his case 
reflect the kind of judgment which must be made in the 
context of the entire case. There is no evidence to suggest 
any additional witnesses, assuming they could be found, 
would have produced a different result, nor is there even 
an inference to be drawn that petitioner suffered actual 
prejudice. Counsel’s performance was not constitu- 
tionally defective. Beavers v. Pruett, 1997 WL 585739 "4 
(4th Cir. September 23, 1997); Beaver v. Thompson, 93 F.3d 
1186, 1195 (4th Cir. 1996). 


Finally, it is clear that in his entire juvenile and short 
adult history, there is not a scintilla of evidence to suggest 
that petitioner suffered from any mental disease or defect 
which would render him unaccountable for his behavior. 


All of the admonitions emanating from Strickland 
apply with particular force to this claim. It is without 
merit. 


254 


3. Petitioner was not entitled to a medical doc- 
tor and an investigator (Claim 7). 


On December 4, 1992, trial counsel filed three 
motions: (1) a motion for the appointment of additional 
counsel; (2) a motion for an expert investigator; and (3) a 
motion for a physical examination (“to include eye tests, 
hearing tests, and a... . physical examination to deter- 
mine ... whether or not the defendant suffers from 
diabetes, arterioscleroses, or other physical disability.”) 


None of the motions was accompanied by a mem- 
orandum in support thereof nor did any of the motions 


articulate specific facts which would justify the granting 
of the motions. 


On December 8, 1992, the matters came on for hear- 
ing before the court. In addressing the motion for a 
complete physical examination, counsel, after first noting 
his objection that he was unable to proceed ex parte, 
explained that he wanted a complete physical because his 
client “does have sight and hearing defects, and I have 
information that leads me to believe that he may suffer 
from some type of reflexive disorder.” (12/8/92 Tr. at 5.) 


The Commonwealth pointed out that petitioner had 
probably had numerous physicals and that there may 
have been medical records in the institutions which the 
defense could examine. (Id. at 6.) The court, in response, 
noted the lack of information that had been furnished to 
the court to justify such an examination and denied the 
motion. (Id. at 6-7.) At this point, counsel for petitioner 
asserted his theory that petitioner fired the gun as a 
reflexive action, after hearing the first shot fired by 
Wilson. Trial counsel went on to state “I also believe that 


255 


Mr. Ramdass is suffering from what is called muscle- 
boundness, that a twist of the body can require a reflexive 
action in the hand.” (Id. at 8.) The court asked counsel, 
“Idjo you have any medical evidence to support your 
belief,” to which counsel replied “[a]t this time I do not, 
your Honor, because I have not been given any physical 
records.” (Id. at 8.) The motion was denied. (Id. at 9.) 


The court then went on to address the request for an 
expert investigator. Trial counsel acknowledged that he 
had no absolute right to his request, but relied upon Ake 
v. Oklahoma, 470 U.S. 68 (1985) (finding entitlement to 
assistance of mental health expert when sanity is signifi- 
cant trial factor or issue of future dangerousness is used 
by prosecution) and Ross v. Moffitt, 417 U.S. 600 (1974) 
(petitioner entitled to the assistance of counsel to pursue 
appeal as a matter of right). (5/18/93 Tr. at 14-15.) 


In response, counsel for the Commonwealth opposed 
the appointment, citing in support of his opposition Gray 
v. Commonwealth, 356 S.E.2d 157, 166 (Va. 1987); Watkins v. 
Commonwealth, 331 S.E.2d 422 (Va. 1985); Stockton v. Com- 
monwealth, 314 S.E.2d 371 (Va. 1984); Quintana v. Common- 
wealth, 295 S.E.2d 643 (Va. 1982); and Yeatts v. 
Commonwealth, 410 S.E.2d 254, 258 (Va. 1991), all cases in 
which the appointment of an investigator was denied. 
(5/18/93 Tr. at 19-20). The court denied petitioner’s 
motion for the appointment of an expert investigator but 


specifically granted the motion to appoint additional — 


counsel to assist in the defense of the matter. (Jd. at 22.) 
The claim in its entirety is without merit. 


Taking the matters in reverse order, while the request 
for an expert investigator, unsupported by all but the 


256 


most perfunctory of reasons, was denied, the defense 
team was given additional relief, at public expense, in the 
form of an attorney. If the purpose of the investigator was 
to gather documents, interview witnesses, and help pre- 
pare the case, both in response to the charge and in 
mitigation, the appointment of a third attorney in the case 
goes a long way toward satisfying the minimally articu- 
lated need for an expert investigator. 


Further, denial of a private investigator has been 
upheld when a defendant requesting such assistance “has 
offered nothing more than a bare assertion that private 
assistance would have been beneficial to him, which does 
not state a violation of due process.” Gray v. Thompson, 58 
F.3d 59, 77 (4th Cir. 1995) (citing Caldwell v. Mississippi, 
472 U.S. 320, 323-24 n.1 (1985)). 


The request for a physical examination was, likewise, 
correctly refused. Petitioner relies almost exclusively on 
Ake v. Oklahoma, 470 U.S. 68 (1985), which stands for the 
proposition that when an indigent defendant credibly 
raises the issue of his sanity at the time of the commission 
of the offenses with which he is charged, he must be 
accorded the opportunity to pursue that defense by the 
appointment, at state expense, of a mental health expert 
who can previde the requisite assistance. While Ake is 
couched in language addressing an insanity defense, the 
principle that it articulates — that when a defendant raises 
a genuine need for expert assistance in order to be able to 
defend himself against criminal charges, assistance is 
mandated - is a constitutionally sound principle, prem- 
ised on demonstrated need. It does not constitute a 
license for a “fishing expedition,” and defense counsel is 


257 


not entitled to medically troll, in hope of snagging a 
medical excuse. 


In Williams v. Martin, 618 F.2d 1021 (4th Cir. 1980), the 
Fourth Circuit granted the habeas petition of a defendant 
who sought the assistance of a forensic pathologist in a 
murder case based upon the defendant's contention that 
the death of the decedent, wh'ch occurred eight months 
after he was shot, was not proximately caused by the 
defendant. In order to establish his claim, it was essential 
that he have expert assistance, but his indigency pre- 
cluded him from acquiring such expertise. Judge Butzner 
stated: 


Because a substantial question requiring expert 
testimony arose over the cause of Maybank’s 
death and because Williams’ defense could not be 
fully developed without professional assistance that 
would have been available to a person who could 
afford his own defense, Williams has satisfied the 
dual test prescribed . . . to establish that he was 
denied equal protection of the law. Further, the 
trial judge’s refusal to provide an expert 
deprived Williams of the effective assistance of 
counsel and due process of law in violation of 
the sixth and fourteenth amendments. 


Id. at 1027 (citations omitted) (emphasis added). 


That case further stands for the proposition that the 
determination of an indigent defendant's need for expert 
assistance is committed to the sound discretion of the 
trial judge. Id. at 1026. See also United States v. Borne, No. 
90-5522, 1991 WL 209832 (4th Cir., October 21, 1991); 
United States v. Briley, No. 86-5619, 1987 WL 37034 (4th 
Cir., April 7, 1987). 


a ee 


258 


The matter was discussed in Terry v. Rees, 985 F.2d 
283 (6th Cir. 1993), in which a defendant sought a 
pathologist to independently determine the cause of 
death. The court concluded that the defendant received a 
fundamentally fair trial. Id. at 284. However, in the course 
of the opinion they discussed the Williams case, conclud- 
ing “[w]e find Williams persuasive and endorse its pro- 
cedure for resolving this issue in this Circuit.” Id. at 285. 


Williams remains good law in the Fourth Circuit. 


Following Ake, the Supreme Court addressed the 
issue of appointment of counsel on appeal and concluded 
that the due process clause of the Fourteenth Amendment 
does not require a state to provide respondent with coun- 


sel in a discretionary appeal to the.state supreme court. 
The court noted: 


An indigent defendant seeking review in the 
Supreme Court of North Carolina is . . . 
somewhat handicapped in comparison with a 
wealthy defendant who has counsel assisting 
him in every conceivable manner at every stage 
in the proceeding. . . . And the fact that a partic- 
ular service might be of benefit to an indigent 
defendant does not mean that the service is 
constitutionally required. The duty of the State 
under our cases is not to duplicate the legal 
arsenal that may be privately retained by a crim- 
inal defendant in a continuing effort to reverse 
his conviction, but only to assure the indigent 
defendant an adequate opportunity to present 
his claims fairly in the context of the State’s 
appellate process. 


Ross v. Moffitt, 417 U.S. 600, 616 (1974). 


259 


Finally, in Caldwell v. Mississippi, 472 U.S. 320 (1985), 
an incidental issue raised by petitioner’s challenge to his 
conviction for murder pursuant to the Mississippi capital 
punishment statute was the trial court's refusal to 
appoint various experts and investigators to assist him, 
specifically a criminal investigator, a fingerprint expert, 
and a ballistics expert. The Supreme Court, in a footnote, 
stated: 


The State Supreme Court affirmed the denials 
because the requests were accompanied by no 
showing as to their reasonableness. For exam- 
ple, the defendant’s request for a ballistics 
expert included little more than ‘the general 
statement that the requested expert “would be 
of great necessarius witness.” ’ 443 So.2d 806, 
812 (1983). Given that petitioner offered little 
more than undeveloped assertions that the 
requested assistance would be beneficial, we 
find no deprivation of due process in the trial 
judge’s decision. . . . We therefore have no need 
to determine as a matter of federal constitu- 
tional law what if any showing would have 
entitled a defendant to assistance of the type 
here sought. 


Caldwell, 472 U.S. at 323, nl. 


4. Petitioner was not unreasonably denied the 
opportunity to proceed in camera in pursuit 
of his experts (Claim 8). 


Petitioner, by counsel, asserts a constitutionally man- 
dated entitlement to ex parte hearings on his request for 
the appointment of experts, in this particular case, a 
medical doctor and an investigator. 


260 


Petitioner cites no cases which establish the constitu- 
tional entitlement he seeks to impose. 


Virginia law has been consistently applied. The Vir- 
ginia Supreme Court, in deciding this case, relied upon 
its previous ruling in O'Dell v. Commonwealth, 364 S.E.2d 
49 (Va. 1988), and applied the ruling in Ramdass, 437 
S.E.2d at 569, and later in Weeks v. Commonwealth, 460 
S.E.2d 379 (Va. 1994). 


Further, a decision establishing such a constitutional 
right and applying it to petitioner’s case would constitute 
the establishment of a new rule, since the constitutional 
protection petitioner seeks was not in place at the time 
petitioner was tried. Habeas relief is appropriate only if a 
state court considering the claim would have felt com- 
pelled by existing precedent to apply the rule sought. 
Saffle v. Parks, 494 U.S. 484, 488 (1990), applied in Gray v. 
Netherland, 116 S.Ct. 2074, 2083 (1996). Further, in order 
for a new rule to be applied retroactively, the rule must 
fall within one or two exceptions provided in Teague v. 
Lane, 489 U.S. 288 (1989): First, a new rule may be applied 
retroactively if the rule “places a class of private conduct 
beyond the power of the State to proscribe.” Saffle at 494 


(citing Teague, at 311.) That is not the case before the 
Court. 


Second, a new rule may be applied “for ‘watershed 
rules of criminal procedure’ implicating the fundamental 
fairness and accuracy of the criminal proceedings.” Parks 


v. Saffle at 495 (citing Teague at 311.) See Gray, 116 S.Ct. at 
2084-85. 


261 


If entitlement to an ex parte hearing on a request for 
expert assistance for an indigent defendant is constitu- 
tionally mandated, and it constitutes a “watershed rule of 
criminal procedure,” such a declaration will have to be 
made by the Court of Appeals or the Supreme Court. The 
claim is without merit. 


5. Petitioner was not unconstitutionally 
denied exculpatory material by the prosecu- 
tion (Claim 9). 


This claim has two components. The first is the 
denial of polygraph results which trial counsel sought to 
use for impeachment purposes. The second component 
consists of statements made by petitioner’s co-defendants 
and other prospective witnesses, to the police, including 
inculpatory and inconsistent statements made by co- 
defendants. 


There is no general constitutional right to discovery 
in a criminal case in Virginia. Lowe v. Commonwealth, 239 
S.E.2d 112 (Va. 1977). Strickler v. Commonwealth, 404 S.E.2d 
227 (Va. 1991); Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 437 S.E.2d 566 
(Va. 1993). Rule 3A:11, Rules of the Supreme Court of 
Virginia addresses and provides for limited discovery in 
criminal cases, setting forth a specific category of mate- 
rials to which a defendant is entitled. Beyond the entitle- 
ment created by the rules, a defendant has no rights. 


Examination of the record in this case, including the 
motions filed by petitioner and the transcripts of the 
motion hearings prior to trial, reveal that extensive dis- 
covery was furnished to petitioner and that the Common- 
wealth was scrupulous in according to petitioner access 


—- a tan. 


262 


to all matters bearing directly on his guilt or innocence 
prior to trial. This Court has no question that the require- 
ments of Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83 (1963) were 
appropriately honored. 


Addressing the specific matters before the Court, 
petitioner concedes that he was not entitled to the results 
of the polygraph examinations of the co-defendants. 
(Reply Pet. at 83.) First, such material is inadmissible 
under Virginia law, Robinson v. Commonwealth, 341 S.E.2d 
159, 167 (1986). Second, failure to disclose polygraph 
results does not deprive a defendant of “material” evi- 
dence under the Brady rule when the polygraphs results 
are inadmissible, even for impeachment purposes, as in 
the Commonwealth of Virginia. Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 
U.S. 1 (1995). 


Petitioner’s remaining claims are likewise without 
merit. First, all of petitioner’s co-defendants in the 
offenses for which he was tried, and in offenses occurring 
in other jurisdictions, were known to or available to 
counsel for the defense, and the witnesses could have 
been called on petitioner’s behalf, had counsel so desire. 
Second, in order for a Brady violation to occur, the mate- 
rial sought must be “material.” Evidence is “material” 
under Brady “only where there exists a ‘reasonable proba- 
bility’ ” that had the evidence been disclosed the result at 
trial would have been different. Wood at 5. A “reasonable 
probability” that the result would have been different is 
shown when the actions of the government “undermine| | 
confidence in the outcome of the trial.” Kyles v. Whitley, 
514 U.S. 419, 434 (1995), quoting United States v. Bagley, 
473 U.S. 667, 678 (1985). 


263 


Further, Brady requires only that the government dis- 
close evidence not available to the defense from other 
sources either directly or through diligent investigation. 
Barnes v. Thompson, 58 F.3d 971, 975 (4th Cir. 1995); Stock- 
ton v. Murray, 41 F.3d 920, 927 (4th Cir. 1994); United 
States v. Wilson, 901 F.2d 378, 380 (4th Cir. 1990). 


Counsel has not asserted either that material was 
withheld from them that they had no other means of 
obtaining or that material was withheld from them that 
would undermine the confidence in the outcome of this 
trial. 


There is no evidence in the record that a Brady viola- 
tion occurred. At least one victim and two co-defendants 
testified against petitioner; his identity as the person who 
committed the robbery, held the gun, and shot Kayani 
was established beyond a reasonable doubt. Assuming, 
arguendo, that the purported Brady material sought, not 
counting the polygraph material, was exculpatory in 
nature, petitioner has made no “showing that the favor- 
able evidence could reasonably be taken to put the whole 
case in such a different light as to undermine confidence 
in the verdict.” Kyles, 514 U.S. at 435. 


Finally, the trial court did not commit error by refus- 
ing to examine the prosecution’s file in camera to deter- 
mine the presence of exculpatory material. In addressing 
the issue, the Virginia Supreme Court said 


In order to establish that the Commonwealth 
has violated a defendant's Brady rights, the 
record must indicate that the undisclosed evi- 
dence was in fact exculpatory and material 
either to guilt or to punishment. . . . Nothing in 


264 


this record indicates either that the Common- 
wealth’s Attorney has withheld exculpatory 
information or that the answers given by Ram- 
dass’s accomplices in their polygraph tests 
would be favorable to him and material to his 
guilt or punishment. 


Ramdass, 437 S.E.2d at 570. 


In Rowe v. Grizzard, 591 F. Supp. 389 (E.D. Va. 1984), 
defense counsel asked for co-defendants’ and witness’s 
statements “which contradicted others’ statements and/ 
or were internally inconsistent.” Id. at 398: The court 
refused, noting the prosecution’s burden to comply with 
Brady. The district court affirmed, noting “[a]bsent a more 
specific request than was made, the trial court is not 


required to make an in camera inspection of the prosecu- 
tion’s entire file.” Id. 


6. Petitioner’s claim that the statutory review 
of his death penalty is insufficient is both 
procedurally barred and unsupported by 
the record (Claim 13). 


Respondent argues that this claim is procedurally 
barred under Slayton, and the Court agrees. 


Petitioner argues that the proportionality review 
required by section 17-110.1(C)(2) of the Virginia Code is 
insufficient for a variety of reasons, the most significant 
of which addresses the alleged insufficiency of the com- 
parisons made by the Supreme Court in assessing 
whether the penalty imposed upon petitioner is consis- 
tent with cases bearing similar facts. There is very little 
legal authority cited for the position taken by petitioner, 


265 


and much of the argument is unsupported. However, in 
addition to attacking the character of the review con- 
ducted by the Supreme Court, petitioner also asserts, 


The state court had a duty under Virginia Code 
section 17-110.1(C) to scrutinize the entire trial 
record for the influence of passion, prejudice, or 
any other arbitrary factor, in addition to allega- 
tions of prejudice raised by counsel. In spite of 
this mandate, the state court ignored ample evi- 
dence of passion, prejudice, and arbitrariness in 
the verdict. Despite the numerous examples of 
passion, prejudice, and arbitrariness noted 
throughout this petition, all of which is incorpo- 
rated herein by reference, the Virginia Supreme 
Court failed to perform any meaningful review 
of the record for such conduct. 


(Pet. { 349.) 


Section 17-110.1(c)(2) mandates that the Supreme 
Court consider and determine “[w]hether the sentence of 
death was imposed under the influence of passion, preju- 
dice, or any other arbitrary factor; and 2. Whether the 
sentence of death is excessive or disproportionate to the 
penalty imposed in similar cases, considering both the 
crime and the defendant.” Va. Code Ann. { 17-110.1(c)(2) 


(Michie 1996). 


There are, however, no examples of “passion, preju- 
dice, and arbitrariness” cited in support of the argument 
nor any suggestion of excessiveness when compared to 


their cases. 
On January 30, 1993, following receipt of the verdict, 


trial counsel made no motion to set aside the verdict as a 
product of passion, prejudice, or arbitrariness. 


266 


On March 2, 1993, when trial counsel appeared and 
moved for a continuance of the hearing procedure 
because petitioner had not yet received all of the records 
to which he was entitled from the state institutions, no 
such motion was made. 


On April 2, 1993, when petitioner and his trial coun- 
sel appeared for sentencing, trial counsel devoted much 
of the hearing to the constitutionality of electrocution, 
and more specifically, the means by which electrocution 
in Virginia is carried out, arguing that both the act, and 
the means implemented in Virginia, were unconstitu- 
tional in violation of the Eighth and Fourteenth Amend- 
ments. However, there was no mention of counsel's 
concern that the verdict was a reflection of passion, preju- 
dice, or arbitrariness. 


In the errors assigned on direct appeal, the matter 
was never raised, a fact noted by the Supreme Court. 
Ramdass, 437 S.E.2d at 573. 


In the state habeas corpus petition, as in this federal 
habeas petition, the issue of the sufficiency of the propor- 
tionality review was raised only in the context of the 
nature of the comparisons engaged in by the Virginia 
Supreme Court and not in the context of a verdict which 
was the product of passion, prejudice, or arbitrariness at 
any time during the trial. 


Finally, this Court has read all of the trial transcripts 
and all of the court documents that were filed by trial 
counsel, reviewed all of the exhibits admitted into evi- 
dence, and read all of the reports filed by experts and 
relied upon in the course of trial. 


267 


If ever a capital murder case was conducted by a 
judge sensitive to the emotional nature of such a proceed- 
ing, this is the trial. The proceeding was clean, to the 
point of being almost antiseptic. Arguments by the pros- 
ecution and the defense were devoid of the kind of emo- 
tional appeals that are frequently presented in conscious 
efforts to inflame a jury’s passions to return a particular 
verdict. 


Instead, the proceeding was objective. The Supreme 
Court of Virginia addressed the nature of the review it 
conducted, reciting petitioner’s criminal history. It stated, 
“we have examined the records in all capital murder 
cases accumulated by this Court . . . paying particular 
attention to those cases in which the underlying felony 
was robbery and the death penalty was based solely on 
‘future dangerousness.’ Ramdass, 437 S.E.2d at 573. Their 
conclusion, following a detailed review of petitioner’s 
criminal history, was that the sentence was neither exces- 
sive nor disproportionate. See Peterson v. Murray, 904 F.2d 
882, 887 (4th Cir. 1990). 


Although the Virginia capital sentencing statute 
requires a proportionality review, the federal constitution 
does not. Buchanan v. Angelone, 103 F.3d 344, 351 (4th Cir. 
1996), citing Pulley v. Harris, 465 U.S. 37, 50-51 (1984). 


In Buchanan, the court went on to say “[t}he Virginia 
Supreme Court conducted the mandatory statutory 
review in this case. . . . On collateral review, federal 
courts are not required to re-examine a state court’s good 
faith findings.” Id. 


When considered in the context of Bennett v. 
Angelone, 92 F.3d 1336 (4th Cir. 1996), a capital murder 


268 


case marked by the highly inflammatory argument of the 
prosecuting attorney, relying upon biblical references to 
behavior and alluding to unrelated grisly murders, the 
case before the Court is exceedingly clean and supports 
the finding of the Virginia Supreme Court that the 
“record discloses nothing to suggest that Ramdass’s 
death sentence was imposed under the influence of pas- 


sion, prejudice, or any other arbitrary factor.” Ramdass 
437 S.E.2d at 427. 


7. The Vir inia death penal statutes, neither 
on their face nor as a lied, violate the 
Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth Amendments, 


and the Virginia capital murder sentencing 
Statute is constitutional (Claims 10, 14). 


Both of these claims focus on the same issue and are 
addressed jointly herein. 


The claims fall generally into broad categories. 


Petitioner alleges: 


a. A jury is given little guidance and not told 
what they must find nor are they given any 
instruction on how to consider the mitigat- 
ing evidence. ({{ 303, 304, 305; see also 
11 351, 352, 353, 356-58, 360-62, 364.) 


b. The aggravating factor of “future danger- 
ousness” is unconstitutionally vague, and 
the penalty is imposed in an unconstitu- 


tional manner. ({¥ 306, 308, 353 
356-358.) aaa 


3 Imposition of the death penalty is uncon- 
stitutional “because it allows but does not 


269 


require that the sentencing court set aside a 
death sentence upon a showing of good 
cause and permits the court to consider 
hearsay in the post-sentence report.” 
(47 309 and 355 essentially sets forth the 
same claim.) 


d. The sentencing jury is permitted to consider 
petitioner’s unadjudicated criminal behav- 
ior. (This ground is discussed separately 
hereinafter.) 


e. The jury was improperly instructed by the 
court, at one stage, when the trial judge 
advised them that they must find beyond a 
reasonable doubt the “possibility” that peti- 
tioner would pose a danger to the public, as 
opposed to the “probability.” ({{ 366-69.) 


We begin with the proposition that “the punishment 
of death does not invariably violate the Constitution.” 
Gregg v. Georgia, 428 U.S. 153, 169 (1976). In that case, 
Justice Stewart said “[flor nearly two centuries, this 
Court, repeatedly and often expressly, has recognized 
that capital punishment is not invalid per se.” Id. at 
177-78. He went on to note that retribution and the possi- 
bility of deterrence are not impermissible considerations 
for a legislature, id. at 183, 185, and then addressed the 
real concern reflected in the decision of Furman v. Georgia, 
408 U.S. 238 (1972), holding the then-existing Georgia 
death penalty statute unconstitutional: 


[T]he concerns expressed in Furman that the 
penalty of death not be imposed in an arbitrary 
or capricious manner can be met by a carefully 
drafted statute that ensures that the sentencing 
authority is given adequate information and 


270 


guidance. As a general proposition these con- 
cerns are best met by a system that provides for 
a bifurcated proceeding at which the sentencing 
authority is apprised of the information relevant 
to the imposition of sentence and provided with 
standards to guide its use of the information. 


Gregg at 195. 


The Court went on to approve a Georgia capital 
sentencing procedure which included, among other 
things, a narrowing of the class of murderers subject to 
capital punishment; specification of certain aggravating 
circumstances, at least one of which must be found by the 
jury to exist before a death sentence can be imposed; 
authorization for a jury to consider any other “appropri- 
ate aggravating or mitigating circumstances,” id. at 197; a 
statutory provision that the jury “is not required to find 
any mitigating circumstance in order to make a recom- 
mendation of mercy that is binding on the trial court,” id_; 
an automatic appeal of a death sentence, id. at 198; and 
mandatory proportional review, id. In short, the Supreme 
Court approved in Gregg the kind of proceeding retlected 
in the Virginia statute. 


On the same day Gregg was announced, the Court 
handed down its decision in Jurek v. Texas, 428 U.S. 262 
(1976), a Texas death penalty case in which the Texas 
statute required that a jury find either that the conduct of 
the defendant causing the death was committed deliber- 
ately and with reasonable expectation that the death 
would result; or that it was probable that the defendant 
would commit criminal acts of violence constituting a 
continuing threat to society; or, if raised by the evidence, 
that defendant’s conduct was an unreasonable response 


271 


to the provocation, if any, by the deceased, as a condition 
for imposing the death penalty. 


Of particular interest, in the context of this case, is 
the second aggravating factor: whether there is a proba- 
bility that the defendant would commit criminal acts of 
violence that would constitute a continuing threat to soci- 


ety. Jurek at 269. 


The Virginia statute essentially parallels the Texas 
statute: 


The penalty of death shall not be imposed 
unless the Commonwealth shall prove beyond a 
reasonable doubt that there is a probability 
based upon evidence of the prior history of the 
defendant or of the circumstances surrounding 
the commission of the offenses of which he is 
accused that he would commit criminal acts of vio- 
lence that would constitute a continuing serious 


threat to society .. . 


Va. Code Ann. § 19.2-264.4(C) (Michie 1995) (emphasis 
added). 


In addressing the attacks on that aggravating ele- 
ment, the Supreme Court discussed the issue of “vague- 


ness”: 


Focusing on the second statutory question that 
Texas requires a jury to answer in considering 
whether to impose a death sentence, the peti- 
tioner argues that it is impossible to predict 
future behavior and that the question is so 
vague as to be meaningless. It is, of course, not 
easy to predict future behavior. The fact that 
such a determination is difficult, however, does 


272 


not mean that it cannot be made. Indeed, predic- 
tion of future criminal conduct is an essential 
element in many of the decisions rendered 
throughout our criminal justice system. The 
a whether to admit a defendant to bail, 
or instance, must often turn on a judge’ 
diction of the defendant's future na And 
any sentencing authority must predict a con- 
victed person’s probable future conduct when it 
engages in the process of determining what 
punishment to impose. For those sentenced to 
prison, these same predictions must be made by 
parole authorities. The task that a Texas jury 
must perform in answering the statutory ques- 
tion in issue is thus basically no different from 
the task performed countless times each day 
throughout the American system of criminal jus- 
tice. What is essential is that the jury have 
before it all possible relevant information about 
the individual defendant whose fate it must 
determine. Texas law clearly assures that all 
such evidence will be adduced. 


Id. at 274-76. 


The concern that a lay jury might not understand the 
term “future dangerousness” was addressed in a concur- 
ring opinion by Justice Blackman who said “I agree with 
Justices Stewart, Powell, and Stevens that the issues 
posed in the sentencing proceeding have a common-sense 
core meaning and that criminal juries should be capable 
of understanding them.” Id. at 279. 


Jurek was applied to the Virginia statute in Peterson v. 
Murray, 904 F.2d 882, 885 (4th Cir. 1990), and the statute 
was found to pass constitutional muster. Briley v. Bass 
750 F.2d 1238, 1245 (4th Cir. 1984). 


273 


Since Peterson and Jurek, the Supreme Court has 
handed down the decision in Tuilaepa v. California, 512 
U.S. 967 (1994), and cautioned reviewing courts that 


Because “the proper degree of definition” of 
eligibility and selection factors often “is not sus- 
ceptible of mathematical precision,” our vague- 
ness review is quite deferential. . . . Relying on 
the basic principle that a factor is not uncon- 
stitutional if it has some “common-sense core of 
meaning . . . that criminal juries should be capa- 
ble of understanding,” . . . we have found only a 
few factors vague... . 


Id. at 973-74 (citations omitted). 


One of them is not “future dangerousness” as 
applied in Virginia. 

An essential element in the issue of future danger- 
ousness is the ability of the jury to consider not only 
aggravating, but mitigating factors. In section 
19.2-264.4(B) of the Virginia Code, the Virginia statute 
specifically permits the jury to consider everything that 
might be a factor in mitigation, and the specific instruc- 
tion given to the jury in this case told the jury that 
mitigating evidence was “evidence which may serve for a 
sentence less than death,” and “may include . . . any facts 
relating to the Defendant’s age, character, education, 
environment, mental condition, life and background, or 
any aspect of the crime itself, which might be considered 
extenuating or tend to reduce his moral culpability, or 
make him less deserving of the extreme punishment of 
death.” (1/29/93 Tr. at 254.) The jury was then told, 
“[y]ou are permitted to fix the punishment of the Defen- 
dant at life . . . even if you find unanimously and beyond 


274 


a reasonable doubt . . . a probability that the Defendant 
would commit criminal acts of violence. . . . ” Id. 


Since Jurek and Peterson, the Virginia statute has been 
upheld in Beaver v. Thompson, 93 F.3d 1186, 1191 (4th Cir. 
1996); Gray v. Thompson, 58 F.3d 59, 63 (4th Cir. 1995); 
Tuggle v. Thompson, 57 F.3d 1356, 1362-63 (4th Cir. 1995); 
and Spencer v. Murray, 5 F.3d 758, 764 (4th Cir. 1993). 


The standard was correctly explained, is not vague, 
and is susceptible of a “common sense” interpretation. 


So long as the jury is permitted to consider any 
mitigating evidence, the instructions to the jury, and the 
resulting verdict, pass constitutional muster. Buchanan v. 
Angelone, No. 96-8400 (U.S. January 21, 1998). 


Petitioner also claims that the statute is unconstitu- 
tional because it permits a sentencing court to set aside a 
death sentence upon a showing of good cause and per- 
mits the court to consider hearsay in the post-sentence 
report. Apparently, the complaint is that at some point in 
time, and on a basis for which there is no articulated 
standard, petitioner may receive the benefit of mercy. 


A similar claim was addressed in Gregg where the 
Court noted 


[T]he petitioner focuses on the opportunities for 
discretionary action that are inherent in the pro- 
cessing of any murder case under Georgia law. 
He notes that the state prosecutor has unfettered 
authority to select those persons whom he 
wishes to prosecute for a capital offense and to 
plea bargain with them. Further, at the trial the 
jury may choose to convict a defendant of a 
lesser included offense rather than find him 


275 


guilty of a crime punishable by death, even if 
the evidence would support a capital verdict. 
And finally, a defendant who is convicted and 
sentenced to die may have his sentence com- 
muted by the Governor of the state and the 
Georgia Board of Pardons and Paroles. 


Gregg at 199. 


All of those conditions, and more, apply to petitioner. 
The Commonwealth did not have to prosecute this case 
as a capital case; the prosecutor could have offered peti- 
tioner a plea bargain, giving up his right to pursue a 
capital punishment in return for a plea of guilty; the jury 
could have imposed a life sentence, as opposed to the 
death penalty, without ever articulating its reasons; the 
trial judge, upon receipt of the presentence report, and 
for “good cause” could have set aside the sentence of 
death and imposed a sentence of imprisonment for life 
(section 19.2-264.5 of the Virginia Code); and petitioner 
may at an unforeseen time in the future, receive clemency 
in the form of a commuted sentence. However, as the 
Court went on to note, “[njothing in any of our cases 
suggest that the decision to afford an individual defen- 
dant mercy violates the Constitution.” Id. at 199. 


The claim regarding consideration of hearsay, as it 
may be incorporated in a presentence report, borders on 
the frivolous. 


First, there is no constitutional entitlement to a trial 
free from hearsay evidence. There are specific exceptions 
recognized in the rules of evidence and certain circum- 
stances where hearsay is deemed appropriate (e.g. section 


. 
) 
| 
& 
% 
. 


276 


19.2-299(c) of the Virginia Code). It is mandated by sec- 
tion 19.2-264.5 of the Virginia Code and “shall . . . contain 
a Victim Impact statement.” 


Second, by law, the defendant must be informed of 
all facts and information contained in the report by being 
furnished a copy thereof. Thus, the defendant is afforded 


the opportunity to correct and/or rebut erroneous infor- 
mation. 


Petitioner is not entitled to relief for this claim which 
arises out of the Virginia sentencing procedure. 


Finally, there is one extraordinarily peculiar claim set 
forth by petitioner in the course of attacking the Virginia 
capital murder statute. Paragraph 365 of the petition 
states, “[a]lthough the Virginia capital sentencing statute 
contemplates consideration of mitigating circumstances, 
the emphasis on speculation as to the future dangerous- 
ness of the defendant inhibits rational and deliberate 
consideration of evidence in mitigation and does not 
adequately allow the jury to dispense mercy.” 


It is difficult to fathom the basis of the argument in 
the face of the trial court’s instruction to the jury in clear, 
nontechnical language that even if they unanimously 
found petitioner to be a future danger that they may 
impose a life sentence as opposed to the death penalty. 
(Rec. at 161, Instr. A(S).) The Court can only conclude that 
the assertion is the result of the emotion engendered in 
these cases and not the product of a bona fide concern 


that a Virginia jury is not free ta vote for life as opposed 
to death. 


277 


8. Petitioner was not denied the right of con- 
frontation (Claim 11). 


Petitioner claims that he was denied the right of 
confrontation in the course of cross-examination of Ram- 
irez and Singh, each of whom was a co-defendant and 
participant in the event which is the subject of this peti- 
tion because the trial court sustained prosecution objec- 
tions to certain defense questions. 


First, the fact that the co-defendants testified and that 
petitioner had the opportunity to hear their testimony on 
direct examination, and to cross-examine the witnesses 
following their direct testimony, absolutely rebuts his 
claim that he was not entitled to confront his accusers. 


Second, any decisions which were made with regard 
to the scope of permitted cross-examination constitute 
evidential rulings, not constitutional rulings arising 
under the Sixth Amendment to the United States Consti- 
tution. No litigant, including a death penalty defendant, 
has the unlimited right to conduct an examination of any 
witness called to the stand. Rules of evidence govern all 
of the participants in a trial and apply with equal vigor in 
criminal cases. 


The judgment of the trial court in denying certain 
questions was clearly within the province of the trial 
court and was upheld on appeal by the Virginia Supreme 
Court. Under the doctrine in Sumner v. Mata, 455 U.S. 591 
(1982), this Court is bound to give weight and credence to 
the decision of the state courts applying Virginia substan- 
tive law to the presentation of evidence in the course of a 
trial. 


278 


As the court noted in Estelle v. McGuire, 502 U.S. 62 
(1991), a federal court’s habeas review is not intended to 
address issues of state criminal procedure and evidence. 
Quoting Spencer v. Texas, 385 U.S. 554, 563-64 (1967), the 
court in Estelle said “it has never been thought that such 
cases establish this court as a rulingmaking organ for the 
promulgation of state rules of criminal procedure.” Id. at 
70. See also Spencer v. Murray, 5 F.2d 758, 762 (4th Cir. 
1993) applying Estelle. 


9. The use of unadjudicated criminal miscon- 
duct in the sentencing phase is constitu- 
tionally permitted (Claim 12). 


Petitioner complains about the admission of unad- 
judicated criminal conduct in the penalty phase of the 
trial. Specifically, petitioner was confronted with testi- 
mony in the sentencing phase of two victims of robberies 
that had not yet been tried. Christopher Cissel, a clerk at 
Bragg Towers, an apartment hotel, described petitioner 
robbing him at gunpoint, striking him with a fist, and 
knocking him to the floor with the butt of the pistol. 
(1/29/93 Tr. at 37, 39-40.) Emmanuel Selassie, a cab 
driver, described being robbed and shot at point blank 
range. (1/29/93 Tr. at 70-76.) Singh identified petitioner 
as the assailant. (1/29/93 Tr. at 62.) | 


Such evidence is expressly permitted under the sen- 
tencing statute. Va. Code Ann. § 19.2-264.3:2. The statute 
requires that a defendant be given notice, in writing, of 
the conduct upon which the Commonwealth intends to 
rely. Such notice was given in this case in letter form to 
trial counsel on January 26, 1993. There were no events of 


279 


which trial counsel was notified that were new or surpris- 
ing to trial counsel, consisting, instead, of all of the 
previous conduct in which petitioner had engaged and 
about which counsel was thoroughly informed. There 
was, therefore, no element of surprise. 


The procedure has been specifically approved in Vir- 
ginia. In Gray v. Thompson, 58 F.3d 59 (4th Cir. 1995), the 


Fourth Circuit said: 


We next turn to the question of future danger- 
ousness. Virginia also permits the death penalty 
if the jury “find[s] that there is a probability that 
the defendant would commit criminal acts of 
violence that would constitute a continuing 
serious threat to society.” . . . In considering the 
defendant's potential violence, the Virginia jury 
has never been restricted to proof of adjudicated , 
criminal convictions. To the contrary, the Com- 
monwealth may introduce evidence of criminal 
misconduct for which the defendant has been 
neither tried nor convicted. . . . This rule of 
latitude —- which also allows the defendant to 
present a wide range of mitigating evidence - 
was fashioned pursuant to the Supreme Court's 
instruction that “it [is] desirable for the jury to 
have as much information before it as possible 
when it makes the sentencing decision.” (Quot- 
ing from Gregg v. Georgia, 428 U.S. at 204). 


Gray at 63 (citations omitted). See also Peterson v. Murray, 
904 F.2d 882 (4th Cir. 1990) (Virginia’s statutory scheme 
designed to strictly limit access to juvenile records did 
not prohibit use of defendant's juvenile record in capital 


sentencing.) 
s 


There is no merit to this claim. 


280 


10. Petitioner’s Simmons Claim has Merit 
(Claim 1). 


In January, 1993, applicable Virginia law precluded 
informing a jury, even in response to a request for infor- 
mation, about a defendant's parole status and whether 
defendant was parole eligible. In Mueller v. Common- 
wealth, 422 S.E.2d 380 (Va. 1992), decided only four 
months before petitioner’s trial, the Supreme Court of 
Virginia said: 


Mueller argues that the trial court violated his 
due process rights by refusing to instruct the 
jury that, pursuant to code section 53.1-151(B1), 
he would not be eligible for parole if convicted 
of the rape of Charity Powers, since this would 
be his third rape conviction. He also asserts that 
he should have been allowed the opportunity to 
introduce evidence regarding parole ineligibility 
in mitigation of punishment at the penalty 
phase of the trial. 


We hold that the trial court did not err in its 
rulings here. This Court has held uniformly and 
repeatedly that information regarding parole eli- 
gibility is not relevant for the jury’s consider- 
ation. 


Further, the United States Supreme Court 
has expressly left the determination of this ques- 
tion to the individual states, as a matter of state 
law. California v. Ramos, 463 U.S. 992, 1013-14, 
103 S.Ct. 3446, 3459-60, 77 L.Ed.2 1171 (1983). 
We perceive no reason in the case before us to 
depart from our prior rulings on this subject. 


Mueller, 422 S.E.2d at 394 (citations omitted). 


281 


The court went on to note “[t}hus, this court has 
drawn a clear distinction between the admissibility of a 
defendant's parole history and evidence of his future 
parole eligibility.” Id. at 394-95. 


During the course of trial, the Commonwealth intro- 
duced direct evidence that petitioner was on parole in 
September, 1992, when the predicate crimes were com- 
mitted (1/29/93 Tr. at 85-86); obtained an admission, 
during cross-examination, that petitioner was paroled in 
May and acquired a gun as early as July, 1992; and 
questioned petitioner about parole violations in 1986, 
petitioner’s absconding from parole, and a subsequent 
robbery, while a fugitive, (1/29/93 Tr. at 201-02). Thus, 
petitioner’s performance, as a parolee, was before the 


jury. 


At the time of trial, in January, 1993, petitioner had 
been tried and convicted of two counts of robbery and 
two counts of use of a firearm in the commission of a 
robbery at a Pizza Hut, sentenced by a jury to a term of 
seventy-six years imprisonment, and the court had 
imposed the sentence. (1/29/93 Tr. at 133.) He had also 
been tried and convicted of two counts of robbery and 
one count of the use of a firearm at a Dominos Pizza, and 
was sentenced by the jury to a term of eighteen years, but 
the sentence had not yet been imposed. (1/29/93 Tr. at 
134.) The sentence was imposed on February 18, 1993, 
after conclusion of the trial which is the subject matter of 
this appeal, but before actual imposition of the punish- 
ment by the trial court. 


282 


On January 29, 1992, there were two statutes on the 
books which addressed parole eligibility and length of 
incarceration in cases involving serious criminal offenses. 


First, section 53.1-151(B1) of the Virginia Code states, 
in part, 


Any person convicted of three separate felony 
offenses of (i) murder, (ii) rape or (iii) robbery 
by the presenting of firearms or other deadly 
weapon, or any combination of the offenses 
specified in subdivisions (i), (ii), or (iii) when 
such offenses were not part of a common act, 


transaction or scheme shall not be eligible for 
parole. 


The second statutory provision, section 53.1-151(C) of the 
Virginia Code reads, in part, 


Any person sentenced to life imprisonment for 
the first time shall be eligible for parole after 
serving fifteen years, except that if such sen- 
tence was for a Class I felony violation . . . he 
shall be eligible for parole after serving twenty- 
five years, unless he is ineligible for parole pur- 
suant to Subsection (B1).. . 


A Class I felony is any felony carrying a penalty of death 
or imprisonment for life. 


Because Virginia law prohibited disclosure to the jury 
of information regarding petitioner’s parole eligibility, no 
instruction to that effect was sought. However, in the 
course of deliberating on petitioner’s punishment, the 
jury sent a question to the court: “if the Defendant is 
given life, is there a possibility of parole at some time 
before his natural death?” (1/29/93 Tr. at 278.) 


283 


The immediate response of the Commonwealth was 
to advise the court of the language of the model jury 
instructions “that you are to decide what a just punish- 
ment is and not to concern yourself with what happens 
thereafter,” based upon Virginia Model Jury Instruction 
2.7000. (1/29/93 Tr. at 278.) In response thereto, peti- 
tioner’s counsel made the following statement: 


MR. PIOTROWSKI: Your Honor, obviously | 
don’t think that that answer is sufficient for the 
circumstances. I’m trying to suggest the word- 
ing and I anticipated this question and I hoped 
to have it written out for you, I don’t, I’m trying 
to formulate it in my mind right now. 


1 believe that the Court (sic) should be told 
that they can not concern themselves with what 
will occur after they impose their sentence. 


I feel, however, Your Honor, though that the 
question itself implies that they have a percep- 
tion that if they give a life sentence that he will 
be out in a very short period of time. 


I’m looking for some kind of language that 
would balance out that perception. A suggestion 
that I have here in front of me is —- I wrote as we 
talked, is that “You must not concern yourself 
with matters that will occur after you impose 
your sentence, but you may impose that your 
sentence will be the legal sentence imposed in 
the case.” 


I’m open to any other suggestions. 
(1/29/93 Tr. at 278-79.) 


It is apparent that trial counsel was familiar with 
prevailing Virginia law but appropriately concerned 


284 


about the jury’s “perception” that petitioner would be out 
in a short period of time. 


The court responded by quoting the Virginia Model 
Jury Instruction and stating to counsel “that’s what the 
law is; isn’t it, Mr. Piotrowski?” (Id. at 279.) In response, 
defense counsel acknowledged that that instruction had 
been given in the past but further responded stating, “I 
think a situation like this, when you're talking about a 
death case, when the issue’s this crucial, that something 
more than that is necessary because their concern is 
parole which they aren't allowed to be told about.” 
(1/29/98 Tr. at 280.) 


The court sustained the Commonwealth's objection, 
informed the jury, pursuant to the Virginia Model Jury 
Instruction, that they should impose what they felt was a 
fair and just sentence and not concern themselves with 


the consequences, and the jury responded by sentencing 
petitioner to death. (/d. at 281.) 


It is important to note at this point that trial counsel 
was not obligated to offer or argue for a specific instruc- 
tion, contrary to controlling Virginia law, and counsel 
was not constitutionally defective for failing to anticipate 
a new rule of law. United States v. McNamara, 74 F.3d 514 
(4th Cir. 1996). 


Thereafter, the issue was preserved at every stage of 
the appellate process. 


On direct appeal, petitioner argued that the trial 
court committed error in refusing to respond to the jury 
question in a way that would make the jury aware that he 
would be ineligible for parole upon his conviction of 


285 


capital murder in derogation of the Eighth and Four- 
teenth Amendments to the United States Constitution. 
The assignment was rejected on direct appeal. Ramdass, 
437 S.E.2d at 425-26. 


In a petition seeking a writ of certiorari from the 
United States Supreme Court, the issue was raised in the 
context of the court’s refusal to respond informatively to 
the jury inquiry regarding petitioner's parole status. 


That petition for certiorari was granted, and on 
remand, the sole issue addressed by petitioner was 
“whether the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth 
Amendment was violated by the refusal of the trial judge 
to instruct the jury that the defendant was ineligible for 
parole when the prosecution relied on future dangerous- 
ness to justify imposition of the death penalty.” (App. 
Brief at ii, Appeal to Va. S.Ct. No. 930693.) 


The matter was resolved in a brief opinion issued 
November 4, 1994, in which the Virginia Supreme Court, 
in perfunctory fashion, decided that Simmons was not an 
issue on the basis that petitioner was not parole ineligible 
on January 29, 1993. Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 450 S.E.2d 
360 (1994). About this, more will be said later. 


Thereafter, a second petition for writ of certiorari was 
taken to the Supreme Court of the United States incor- 
porating the same issue. The petition was denied. Ram- 
dass v. Virginia, 514 U.S. 1085 (1995). 


Next, in a state habeas petition, the issue was 
addressed as an assignment of trial court error in the 
specific Simmons context. The claim was rejected on the 
basis of Hawks v. Cox, 175 S.E.2d 271 (Va. 1970), the issue 


286 


having previously been decided by the Virginia Supreme 
Court. 


Thus, the issue has been preserved at every stage and 
is before the Court for consideration. 


Further, since the writ of certiorari was pending in the 
United States Supreme Court at the time Simmons was 
decided, and the petition for certiorari was granted 
post-Simmons, there is no retroactive application 
required, and as to petitioner, the Simmons holding does 
not constitute a new rule. “A state conviction and sen- 
tence become final for purposes of retroactivity analysis 
when the availability of direct appeal to the state courts 
has been exhausted and the time for filing a petition for a 
writ of certiorari has elapsed or a timely filed petition has 
been finally denied.” Caspari v. Bohlen, 510 U.S. 383, 390 
(1994). See also O'Dell v. Netherland, 95 F.3d 1214, 1221 (4th 
Cir. 1996). 


Further, under the doctrine in Teague v. Lane, 489 U.S. 
288, 301 (1989), a new rule is created if the result was not 
dictated by precedent existing at the time petitioner's 
conviction became final. This Court finds that the rule 
articulated in Simmons, “that where the defendant's 
future dangerousness is at issue, and state law prohibits 
the defendant's release on parole, due process requires 
that the sentencing jury be informed that the defendant is 
parole ineligible,” compelled a finding by the Virginia 
Supreme Court, on remand, that petitioner was entitled 
to inform the jury, prior to sentencing, of his parole 
Status. Simmons, 512 U.S. at 156. 


287 


On remand, Justice Whiting, the author of the origi- 
nal opinion following petitioner’s direct appeal, con- 
cluded that petitioner was ineligible for parole, stating: 


At the time that the jury was considering 
Ramdass’s penalty on January 30, 1993, Ram- 
dass was not ineligible for parole since he had 
only two “separate felony offenses” within the 
meaning of Code § 53.1-151(B1). They were: 


(1) Ramdass’s January 22, 1993, convictions 
and sentences for the armed robbery of two 
persons on August 25, 1992, at a Pizza Hut in 
Fairfax County. Since both convictions arose 
from the same transaction, they are considered 
as one felony offense under the express provi- 
sions of Code § 53.1-151(B1). 


(2) Ramdass’s convictions for Kayani’s capital 
murder and robbery arising from the same 
transaction and, thus, considered as one felony 
offense under Code § 53.1-151(B1). 


We do not agree with Ramdass’s contention 
that his January 7, 1993, conviction by a jury of 
an armed robbery in Alexandria also made him 
ineligible for parole under the statute. Judgment 
had not been entered on that verdict; therefore, 
it cannot be considered as a conviction under 
Code § 53.1-151(B1). See Smith v. Commonwealth, 
134 Va. 589, 592-93, 113 S.E. 707, 709 (1922) 
(jury's verdict of conviction upon which no 
judgment had been entered not conviction 
within meaning of statute disqualifying person 
from holding public office). Since Ramdass was 
not ineligible for parole when the jury was 


288 


considering his sentence, the Simmons decision 
does not affect his death sentence. 


Ramdass v. Commonwealth, 450 S.E.2d at 361. 


It did not seem to bother Justice Whiting that on 
January 29, 1993, although judgment had not been 


entered on the robbery or murder of Kayani, petitioner 
had been convicted. 


Petitioner essentially presents four arguments in sup- 
port of the position that the court's failure to allow con- 
sideration of petitioner's parole ineligibility violated his 
Eighth and Fourteenth Amendment rights: first, that the 
Virginia Supreme Court decision was ultra vires, arguing 
that it is for the parole board to determine parole eligi- 
bility; second, that the Virginia Supreme Court decision 
was arbitrary, arguing that it was internally inconsistent, 
ignored Virginia authority, and contravened the plain 
language of the statute; third, that to deny the jury the 
information violates petitioner’s Eighth Amendment 
rights; and fourth, that both the trial court and the Vir- 
ginia Supreme Court acted arbitrarily in the actual impo- 
sition of the sentence since, at that point in time, 
petitioner had not only been convicted of the predicate 
offenses involved in the trial which is the subject of this 
action, but had been tried and sentenced for a fourth 


robbery at the Bragg Hotel and was, thus, unquestionably 
ineligible for parole. 


- The respondent treats the Simmons claim as de min- 
imis. 


In a two and one-half page reply, one full page is 
devoted to a long quote from the opinion. The respondent 


289 


relies almost exclusively upon the argument that the find- 
ing of parole ineligibility is a finding of historical fact 
entitled to a presumption of correctness, citing Sumner v. 
Mata, 449 U.S. 539 548-49 (1981), and concludes that peti- 
tioner’s arguments, which are different from those pre- 
sented to the Virginia Supreme Court, are now barred 
from review. The state seems to take great comfort in the 
fact that upon remand, when petitioner’s counsel were 
entitled to file a reply brief in response to the Common- 
wealth’s argument that petitioner was not ineligible for 
parole, they did not do so. However, until the Supreme 
Court determined, in fact, that petitioner was not eligible 
for parole, there was no issue of parole eligibility to 
which petitioner was obligated to respond. After all, the 
prosecutor never suggested to the trial court that peti- 
tioner was parole ineligible in the course of objecting to a 
response to the ju.y’s question in language other than 
that provided by the Virginia Model Jury Instructions. 


Until the Virginia Supreme Court determined that 
petitioner wa. parole ineligible, the issue did not exist. 


Petitioner’s reply brief essentially repeats the argu- 
ments raised in the petition, except for an additional 
argument that if there are any defaulted claims that per- 
tain to the Simmons issue and which were not raised by 
state habeas counsel, petitioner argues that state habeas 
counsel were constitutionally ineffective, and petitioner is 
entitled to raise such matters herein. The issue is moot. 
The case upon which petitioner relies, MacKall v. Murray, 
109 F.3d 957 (4th Cir. 1997), which suggested entitlement 
to effective assistance of counsel in the first habeas pro- 
ceeding, was vacated in MacKall v. Angelone, 131 F.3d 442, 
449 (1997), when the Fourth Circuit said, “[blecause 


290 


MacKall has no right to effective assistance of counsel in 
his state habeas proceedings, he cannot demonstrate 
cause to excuse the procedural default of his claims that 
his trial and appellate counsel were constitutionally inef- 


fective. Consequently federal habeas review of those 
claims is barred.” 


The respondent's reply adds nothing to the argu- 
ment. 


Simmons is an extremely important constitutional 
case. It establishes a principle that goes beyond the nar- 
row nature of the holding. In Simmons, the sole question 
was whether a defendant, facing the death penalty and 
then statutorily ineligible for parole under state law, had 
the right to inform the jury of his parole ineligibility 
when the state relied upon his alleged future dangerous- 
ness in seeking the death penalty. 


Simmons represents the most recent in a series of 
cases incrementally refining the process of applying the 
death penalty by expanding, first, the sentencer’s knowl- 
edge, and second, an accused's right to respond to argu- 


ments advanced by the sovereign for the imposition of 
the death penalty. 


In Gregg v. Georgia, 428 U.S. 153 (1976), Justice Stew- 
art said: 


If an experienced trial judge, who daily faces the 
difficult task of imposing sentences, has a vital 
need for accurate information about a defendant 
and the crime he committed in order to be able 
to impose a rational sentence in the typical crim- 
inal case, then accurate sentencing information 
is an indispensable prerequisite to a reasoned 


291 


determination of whether a defendant shall live 
or die by a jury of people who may never before 
have made a sentencing decision. 


Id. at 190. 


In Gardner v. Florida, 430 U.S. 349 (1977), petitioner 
was sentenced to death based upon an aggravating factor 
contained in a presentence investigation report that the 
Court had ordered, but portions of which were not dis- 
closed to or requested by counsel for the parties. In 
setting aside the conviction, the Court stated, succinctly, 
“Iwle conclude that petitioner was denied due process of 
law when the death sentence was imposed, at least in 
part, on the basis of information which he had no oppor- 
tunity to deny or explain.” Id. at 362. 


In Lockett v. Ohio, 438 U.S. 586 (1978), a death penalty 
was set aside because the limited range of mitigating 
circumstances that could be considered by the sentencer 
under the Ohio death penalty statute was deemed to be 
incompatible with the Eighth and Fourteenth Amend- 
ments. The Court concluded that except in the most 
unusual of circumstances, a sentencer may not be pre- 
cluded from considering as a mitigating factor any aspect 
of a defendant's character or record and any of the cir- 
cumstances of the offense proffered as a basis for a sen- 
tence less than death. 


In Eddings v. Oklahoma, 455 U.S. 104 (1982), the trial 
court refused, as a matter of law, to consider in mitigation 
circumstances concerning defendant's childhood and cer- 
tain emotional disturbances, and found that the only 
mitigating circumstance was his youth. In setting aside 
the sentence, Justice Powell said 


met a AD nal tall Bilge Mls lisa ate iting Se aa 


Sal aah b lear. Oa tae t vi ON barge 


292 


We find that the limitations placed by these 
courts upon the mitigating evidence they would 
consider violated the rule in Lockett. Just as the 
State may not by statute preclude the sentencer 
from considering any mitigating factor, neither 
may the sentencer refuse to consider, as a matter 
of law, any relevant mitigating evidence. 


Id. at 113-14. 


In California v. Ramos, 463 U.S. 992 (1993), the Court 
addressed the issue of whether it was constitutional to 
require that the trial judge inform the jury that a sentence 
of life imprisonment without the possibility of parole 
may be commuted by the governor to a sentence that 
includes the possibility of parole, thus informing the jury 
that a prisoner sentenced to life without parole could 
eventually gain freedom. In permitting the disclosure, the 
jury received nothing more than an accurate statement of 
California law. In upholding the right of the state of 
California to so inform a jury, the court stated “[t}he 
Briggs Instruction gives the jury accurate information of 
which both the defendant and his counsel are aware, and 
it does not preclude the defendant from offering any 


evidence or argument regarding the Governor’s power to 
commute a life sentence.” Id. at 1004 


In Barclay v. Florida, 463 U.S. 939 (1983), petitioner 
was sentenced to death by the trial court, contrary to the 
jury’s advisory recommendation that petitioner be sen- 
tenced to life. In written findings, the trial judge con- 
cluded that petitioner’s behavior in the course of 
committing the crimes, and the nature of the crimes 
themselves, all of which were aggravating factors, were 
not offset by any mitigating factors and that the death 


293 


penalty was theo ~— apprepriate sentence. The judgment 
was ultimately affirmed by the Florida Supreme Court 
and subsequently by the United States Supreme Court. In 
so doing, Justice Rehnquist noted that “[t]he United 
States Constitution does not prohibit a trial judge from 
taking into account the elements of racial hatred in this 
murder.” Id. at 949. The Chief Justice went on to state: 


Any sentencing decision calls for the exercise of 
judgment. It is neither possible nor desirable for 
a person to whom the State entrusts an impor- 
tant judgment to decide in a vacuum, as if he 
had no experiences. The thrust of our decisions 
on capital punishment has been that “discretion 
must be suitably directed and limited so as to 
minimize the risk of wholly arbitrary and capri- 


cious action.” 


Id. at 950 (quoting Zant v. Stephens, 462 U.S. 8962, 874 
(1983)). 


In McCleskey v. Kemp, 481 U.S. 279 (1987), the Court 
said, “States cannot limit the sentencer’s consideration of 
any relevant circumstance that could cause it to decline to 
impose the penalty. In this respect, the State cannot chan- 
nel the sentencer’s discretion, but must allow it to con- 
sider any relevant information offered by the defendant.” 


Id. at 306. 


Finally, prior to Simmons, Skipper v. South Carolina, 
476 U.S. 1 (1986) was decided. In that case, the trial court 
ruled that evidence that the defendant has made a good 
adjustment to confinement was irrelevant and inadmiss- 
able, and the testimony of two jailors [sic] and a regular 
visitor to the jail to that effect was excluded by the trial 


" Saeed ee dy 


294 


court. Thereafter, the defendant was sentenced to death 
In reversing the conviction, the Supreme Court said 


The evidence petitioner was allowed to present 
on the issue of his conduct in jail was the sort of 
evidence that a jury naturally would tend to 
discount as self-serving. The testimony of more 
disinterested witnesses — and, in particular, of 
jailers who would have had no particular reason 
to be favorably predisposed toward one of their 
charges - would quite naturally be given much 
greater weight by the jury. Nor can we confi- 
dently conclude that credible evidence that peti- 
tioner was a good prisoner would have had no 
effect upon the jury’s deliberations. The pros- 
ecutor himself, in closing argument, made much 
of the dangers petitioner would pose if sen- 
tenced to prison, and went so far as to assert 
that petitioner could be expected to rape other 
inmates. Under these circumstances, it appears 
reasonably likely that the exclusion of evidence 
bearing upon petitioner’s behavior in jail (and 
hence, upon his likely future behavior in prison) 
may have affected the jury’s decision to impose 
the death sentence. Thus, under any standard 
the exclusion of the evidence was sufficiently 
prejudicial to constitute reversible error. 


Id. at 8. 


__ Thus, we arrive at Simmons. In Simmons, as in the 
case before the Court, the state relied on future danger- 
ousness as a factor for the jury to consider in determining 
whether Simmons should be sentenced to death or given 
life imprisonment for the murder of an elderly woman. 
Simmons, 512 U.S. at 157. Petitioner in that case presented 
evidence that his future dangerousness was limited to 


295 


elderly women and, therefore, the jury would have no 
reason to expect violent acts from him in prison. How- 
ever, he was unable to persuade the trial court to give the 
jury an instruction which advised them that he was inel- 
igible for parole under South Carolina law. Id. at 158. 


During deliberations in the sentencing phase of the 
case, the jury posed the question of whether life 
imprisonment carried with it the possibility of parole. As 
in Virginia, the court instructed the jury not to consider 
parole in reaching its verdict and further said, “the terms 
life imprisonment and death sentence are to be under- 
stood in their plain and ordinary meaning.” Simmons at 
160. Twenty-five minutes later, the jury returned with the 
sentence of death. Id. 


The issue on appeal was whether the jury was enti- 
tled to know that petitioner was, in fact, ineligible for 
parole. In a seven to two opinion, the Court concluded 
that the jury was so entitled. There are five separate 
opinions in the case. Justice Blackman wrote the opinion 
for the Court, in which Justices Stevens, Souter, and
